---
title: "Upon This Winter's Night: The Story of Julian Rowlands"
---

# Era 1: The Winter Palace & The Breach (Spring – December 1996)

## Foreign Language

### The 8th Grade IEP Meeting

**Date:** 1996-04-17 at 14:00 America/New_York

The fluorescent lights in the James City County Middle School guidance office buzzed with a low, grating electrical hum.

Julian sat completely rigid in his manual wheelchair, his hands clamped tightly over his ears. He hated this room. It smelled faintly of stale coffee and industrial floor wax, and the sheer volume of paperwork piled on the counselor's desk was visually overwhelming.

Clara and Maria sat on either side of him in hard plastic guest chairs. They weren't officially part of the Rowlands family, but when it came to Julian's Individualized Education Program (IEP) meetings, the identical twins' presence was legally mandated as a necessary medical accommodation. It was a highly unusual, virtually unheard-of directive that Eira had fought ruthlessly to secure. Clara leaned her shoulder firmly against his left side, and Maria mirrored her on the right, offering a steady, symmetrical grounding pressure.

Across the desk sat Mr. Caldwell, an exhausted guidance counselor who had spent his entire career trying to fit square pegs into round administrative holes.

"Alright," Mr. Caldwell sighed, adjusting his glasses as he looked over the high school transition paperwork. "Moving into the freshman year, Julian needs to select a foreign language track to stay on the advanced diploma path. And girls, your schedules need to mirror his exactly, per the IEP."

"That's correct," Eira stated. She sat perfectly straight in her chair, projecting an aura of absolute, uncompromising authority.

Internally, Eira was running on fumes. It had only been a few months since she had finally managed to force Tom out of the house. Because of Virginia’s strict family laws, she was trapped in the mandatory, agonizing one-year legal separation period before she could officially file for divorce. Tom’s ghost was still legally attached to her, and despite the strict no-contact order keeping him away from the kids, her tolerance for bureaucratic pushback regarding her children was absolutely zero.

Richard Harrison sat in the chair next to her, a quiet, unyielding sentry.

Mr. Caldwell tapped his pen against the file. "Well, this seems like an easy win. The file notes that Julian is already completely bilingual. He speaks French at home. And Clara, Maria, you two have picked it up from being around the family?"

"Yes, sir," the twins answered in perfect, identical unison.

"Perfect," Mr. Caldwell smiled, writing a note on the schedule. "I'll put all three of you down for French I. It'll be an easy 'A' for everyone, and it satisfies the graduation requirement. The high school French teacher, Madame Dupont, is wonderful."

"No," Eira said flatly. The single syllable cut through the buzzing office like a knife.

Mr. Caldwell blinked, his pen freezing over the paper. "Mrs. Cross, I assure you, it's the most logical—"

"My son speaks Québécois French, Mr. Caldwell," Eira interrupted, her Welsh accent clipping with cold precision. "The high school curriculum teaches Metropolitan, Parisian French. The vocabulary, the cadence, and the grammatical structures are vastly different."

"Well, French is French, isn't it?" Mr. Caldwell chuckled weakly, clearly out of his depth. "Madame Dupont will just correct his pronunciation."

Richard leaned forward, resting his forearms on his knees. He didn't raise his voice, but the weight of his tone instantly erased the counselor's smile.

"If a high school teacher stands at the front of a classroom and repeatedly tells an autistic teenager that his native language—the language he uses to feel safe in his own home—is wrong, it will trigger a catastrophic meltdown," Richard explained, laying out the psychological reality with brutal clarity. "French is strictly off the table."

Mr. Caldwell swallowed hard, crossing 'French' off the list. "Alright. Spanish? German? They are highly conversational classes..."

"Exactly the problem," Eira countered. "Modern language curriculums rely heavily on spontaneous oral exams and auditory comprehension tapes. When Julian is overwhelmed, his verbal abilities shut down. He relies on his AAC device. You cannot force him into a highly conversational, unpredictable auditory environment."

Julian dropped his hands from his ears, resting them on his AAC device. He hated the idea of a conversational class. He hated the idea of standing up in front of thirty teenagers and being forced to speak a language he didn't know.

He looked at Clara and Maria. They already knew exactly what he was thinking.

"What about Latin?" Clara asked quietly.

Mr. Caldwell looked up, surprised. "Latin? It's a dead language. Nobody speaks it."

"Exactly," Maria finished, a small, brilliant smile touching the corners of her mouth. She looked at Julian. "No oral exams. No listening to crackling cassette tapes. It's just reading and translating. It's all written down."

Julian’s eyes brightened. He pulled his AAC device onto his lap, his fingers flying across the keys. A language that didn't require talking? A language built entirely on rigid, mathematical grammatical rules? It wasn't a social exercise; it was a puzzle.

<aac>LATIN. IT IS A PUZZLE. I WANT THE PUZZLE.</aac> the robotic voice announced.

Richard smiled proudly, looking at his daughters and the boy they protected. "It satisfies the language requirement for the diploma, doesn't it?"

"Well... yes," Mr. Caldwell admitted, completely outmaneuvered. "The high school offers Latin I through IV."

"Then it's settled," Eira declared, standing up and ending the meeting entirely on her own terms. "Enroll them in Latin I. And Mr. Caldwell? Make sure their desks are placed in the back of the room, three in a row, with Julian in the center."

## The Empty Frame

### The Breach & The Garrison

**Date:** 1996-12-11 at 08:00 America/New_York

#### 8:00 AM • Eira Rowlands — The Breach

The cold December wind howling off the Virginia peninsula usually meant one thing in the Cross household: it was time to lock the deadbolts, turn up the baseboard heaters, and settle into the absolute, predictable routine that kept Julian safe.

Gabriel Cross sat on the edge of his mattress, his calloused fingers absentmindedly running over the fretboard of his acoustic guitar. At eighteen, he was a coiled spring of frontline energy, built broad and solid, naturally assuming the role of the family’s physical shield the moment the ink had dried on his parents' incredibly ugly divorce papers. The house in Ewell was supposed to be a fortress now. The court had stripped their father of all visitation rights. The monster was legally barred from the property.

But Gabriel had always known deep down that a piece of paper wouldn't stop a monster. He had feared his father would try something like this. And now, the perimeter was about to shatter.

The sound didn't register as a word at first. It was a raw, visceral shriek that tore through the drywall from the front of the house, carrying a pitch of pure, unadulterated terror.

Gabriel dropped his guitar. It hit the carpet with a hollow thud as he bolted through his bedroom door, his heavy boots skidding against the hardwood of the hallway.

"Mom?!" Gabriel shouted, his chest heaving as he sprinted into the living room.

Eira Cross was collapsed on her knees near the open front entryway, the freezing winter wind whipping her hair across her face. The corded receiver of the wall phone was dangling from its coiled wire, swinging against the drywall. Eira wasn't dialing. She was just staring out the open doorway at the frost-covered driveway, hyperventilating.

"Mom, what is it? Are you hurt?" Gabriel demanded, his protective instincts flooding his nervous system. He instinctively stepped in front of her, positioning his body to block whatever threat was coming through the door.

But there was no one in the driveway. There were only fresh, frantic tire tracks carving through the morning frost, leading aggressively toward the main road.

"He took him," Eira gasped, her voice breaking into a ragged, breathless sob. "Gabriel... he took him."

Gabriel froze. His eyes darted away from the driveway and snapped to the corner of the living room.

The space by the window was empty. Julian’s heavy, customized manual wheelchair was completely gone. But the visual of what was left behind was what made Gabriel's stomach violently drop. Julian's heavy winter coat was still hooked over the back of the sofa, entirely untouched. The weighted sensory blanket he always kept over his lap was crumpled in a heap on the floor, discarded like trash.

"No," Gabriel breathed, the word catching in his throat. The sheer impossibility of it crashed into him. Julian was fourteen. He had Cerebral Palsy. He was completely non-verbal. He relied entirely on the strict, unconditional safety of this exact room. "No, he can't be out there. He doesn't have his coat."

"Your father," Eira wept, her hands shaking uncontrollably as she pointed toward the driveway. "He was waiting by the hedges. When I opened the door to get the mail... he just shoved past me. He grabbed Julian right out of his seat. He carried him to the car, and then he came running back. He grabbed the manual chair and shoved it haphazardly into the trunk like it was a piece of luggage! He just slammed it shut and drove away!"

A blinding, white-hot rage detonated in Gabriel’s chest. His hands curled into violent fists at his sides.

"<lang="cy" trans="I knew it,">Roeddwn i'n gwybod,</lang>" Gabriel snarled under his breath, a stream of furious, jagged Welsh spilling from his lips as he paced frantically near the door. "<lang="cy" trans="I knew the monster would try something! I should have been at the door!">Roeddwn i'n gwybod y byddai'r anghenfil yn ceisio rhywbeth! Dylwn i fod wedi bod wrth y drws!</lang>"

He wanted to punch straight through the doorframe. He wanted to run down the asphalt and tear the engine out of his father's car with his bare hands. He was the older brother. He was supposed to block the doorway. He was supposed to be the shield. But he had been in his bedroom playing a guitar, completely oblivious while his disabled brother was ambushed and violently ripped from his sanctuary.

Footsteps echoed sharply from the kitchen. Gwendolyn rounded the corner, taking in the open door, their mother on the floor, and the empty space where the wheelchair belonged.

Unlike Gabriel’s explosive, physical fury, Gwendolyn’s reaction was a terrifying, absolute freeze. Her sharp, observant eyes calculated the scene in a fraction of a second.

"How long ago?" Gwendolyn asked, her voice dropping into a cold, tactical register as she knelt beside their mother, ignoring the freezing wind. "Mom, look at me. How many minutes?"

"Two," Eira choked out, gripping Gwendolyn’s arm. "Maybe three. I couldn't stop him, Gwen."

Gabriel spun around, his chest heaving, his Welsh tirade cutting off. "I'm getting in the car. I can catch him."

"No, you can't," Gwendolyn snapped, standing up to block his path. "Gabriel, look at the tracks. He didn't go toward the county route. He went toward the interstate. By the time you get your keys, he’ll be on the highway. We don't even know if he went north or south."

"I have to do something!" Gabriel roared, his voice cracking with the sheer, crushing weight of his helplessness. The image of Julian—terrified, freezing without his coat, entirely overstimulated, and trapped in the back of a speeding car with his abuser—was an agony so profound it felt like a physical wound.

Gabriel lunged for the wall, snatching the swinging phone receiver out of the air. His furious Welsh instantly evaporated, locked away as he forced himself into the rigid, terrifyingly necessary English of the outside world. He hammered the three digits into the keypad with a shaking finger.

The line rang twice before a voice crackled through the earpiece. "9-1-1, what is your emergency?"

Gabriel gripped the plastic receiver so hard it groaned. "My brother has just been abducted. It's a non-custodial kidnapping. He is fourteen, he is non-verbal, and he is physically disabled. The man who took him is driving toward the interstate, and my brother is in immediate medical danger."

The monster had broken through their perimeter, and the quiet, festive winter they had promised Julian was entirely, devastatingly gone.

***

#### 8:05 AM • Gabriel Rowlands — The Beacon

Gabriel slammed the heavy plastic receiver of the wall phone back into its cradle. The sharp clack echoed like a gunshot in the freezing living room.

Eira remained collapsed against the linoleum of the entryway, her knees pulled to her chest as the bitter December wind whipped through the open front door. Gabriel stepped over and gently, firmly pulled the heavy wooden door shut, engaging the deadbolt. Closing it felt like a sickening defeat—like locking Julian on the outside—but the house was dropping to a freezing temperature, and his brother wasn't coming back up the driveway.

"<lang="cy" trans="He is on the interstate by now,">Mae e ar y draffordd erbyn hyn,</lang>" Gabriel muttered, his voice a low, vibrating growl as he began to pace the length of the living room. The English language was entirely discarded, replaced by the jagged, rapid-fire Welsh that fueled his frontline fury. "<lang="cy" trans="Five minutes head start. At sixty-five miles an hour, he is already out of the county.">Pum munud o flaen llaw. Ar chwe deg pump milltir yr awr, mae e eisoes y tu allan i'r sir.</lang>"

Gwendolyn stood by the kitchen island, her sharp, tactical mind ruthlessly calculating the exit vectors. She didn't cry. The analytical freeze had entirely taken over her nervous system, mirroring her twin's native tongue.

"<lang="cy" trans="He wouldn't take the ferry,">Ni fyddai'n cymryd y fferi,</lang>" Gwendolyn fired back, her eyes tracking an invisible map across the wallpaper. "<lang="cy" trans="He wouldn't want to be trapped on a boat. And he wouldn't go north over the toll bridge because of the cameras.">Fyddai e ddim am gael ei ddal ar gwch. Ac ni fyddai'n mynd i'r gogledd dros y bont doll oherwydd y camerâu.</lang>"

"<lang="cy" trans="So he's going west,">Felly mae e'n mynd i'r gorllewin,</lang>" Gabriel concluded, his heavy boots thudding against the carpet. His hands were curled into fists so tight his fingernails bit into his palms. "He's taking him west. He's getting him off the peninsula. He's going to bury him somewhere in the interior where we can't reach him."

"The police are on their way," Eira whispered from the floor, her voice hollow and devastated.

"The police are going to treat this like a domestic dispute first, Mom," Gwendolyn said, the cold, suffocating reality of the legal system crashing down on her. She switched back to English for her mother's sake. "They have to process the paperwork to prove Tom doesn't have custody before they escalate it to a kidnapping. It’s going to take hours. Maybe days."

"We can't wait days," Gabriel snarled, stopping his pacing to stare at the empty space by the window. The visual of the discarded weighted blanket was an agony so profound it felt like a physical wound. "He's terrified, Gwen. He doesn't have his coat. He doesn't have his routine. That monster doesn't care if Julian is in pain or completely overloaded. We have to go after him ourselves."

"How, Gabriel?" Gwendolyn’s voice finally broke, a sharp, desperate sound that completely shattered her tactical facade. "Look around! We are broke. We barely make enough to cover the heating bill. We don't have the thousands of dollars it takes to hire private investigators. We don't have cars fast enough or resources wide enough to comb the entire interstate system. We are entirely cut off!"

Gabriel stared at his twin sister. The sheer, crushing helplessness of being eighteen years old hit him like a freight train. They were just kids. They had spent years building an impenetrable fortress for their brother, and Tom had bypassed it in three minutes.

The silence in the house was deafening. It was a sensory void where Julian's happy, rhythmic vocalizations usually lived.

Gabriel slowly walked back into his bedroom. He looked down at the acoustic guitar he had dropped on the carpet. He picked it up, gripping the wooden neck and feeling the worn, familiar grooves under his calloused fingers. It was the only thing in the house that actually belonged to him, the only tool he knew how to wield with any real power.

Gabriel walked back out to the living room, the guitar strapped across his chest.

"Then we don't look for him," Gabriel said, his voice dropping into a heavy, resonant register. His fierce, protective energy locked onto a new, desperate target. "We make him hear us."

Gwendolyn looked at the acoustic guitar, and then slowly turned her head toward the upright piano sitting in the corner of the room. The tactical gears in her mind violently shifted direction.

"A song," Gwendolyn whispered, the sheer impossibility of the idea sparking a terrifying, brilliant light in her eyes. "If we write it... if we can get it recorded and beg the local radio stations to play it..."

"A beacon," Gabriel finished, stepping toward the piano. The heavy, frontline defense he carried wasn't physical anymore; it was auditory. "Tom will have the radio on in the car. Julian will hear it. He'll feel the vibration of the strings. He'll know we haven't stopped fighting."

Gwendolyn sat down on the wooden piano bench. Her hands hovered over the black and white keys, trembling slightly as she channeled every ounce of her grief and tactical precision into her fingertips.

"It can't just be noise," Gwendolyn commanded, her mind already arranging the strings and the tempo. "It has to start soft. A music box. Something that sounds like his room, to keep him from panicking."

"And then we blow the doors off," Gabriel agreed, his jaw set in absolute determination. He was ready to go to war. "We write it for him. The Winter Palace."

Gwendolyn pressed her fingers into the highest octave of the piano, playing a fragile, plinking, delicate melody that sounded exactly like a child's wind-up toy. It was the quiet, desperate genesis of a rock opera, and the exact moment Upon This Winter's Night was born into the freezing Virginia air.

***

#### 8:20 AM • Gwendolyn Rowlands — The Winter Palace

The heavy, suffocating silence of the living room was finally broken by movement.

Gwendolyn stood up from the piano bench, the rigid, tactical freeze of her posture finally cracking. She crossed the short distance between them and threw her arms around Gabriel’s neck, pulling him into a fierce, desperate hug.

Gabriel hugged her back, his broad frame hunching over her shoulder. For the last ten minutes, they had been entirely paralyzed by the sheer, crushing helplessness of being eighteen-year-old kids without the resources to hunt down a kidnapper. But Gwendolyn’s idea had just handed him a weapon.

"We do it," Gwendolyn whispered fiercely against his heavy flannel shirt. "We record it. We make the local stations play it."

Gabriel nodded, pulling back. His eyes drifted down the hall toward the small, soundproofed spare room they used as a makeshift studio. It was where they usually retreated to isolate themselves, to build their tracks in perfect, obsessive privacy.

"Not in the studio," Gabriel said, his voice a low, resonant rumble. He looked over at their mother, Eira, who was still sitting on the floor by the entryway. She had her knees pulled to her chest, utterly hollowed out by the ambush. "We hash it out right here. In the living room. Mom needs to hear it. She needs to know we aren't just sitting here waiting for the police to file domestic paperwork."

Gwendolyn followed his gaze and nodded in absolute agreement. She walked over to Eira, gently guiding their mother up from the cold linoleum. She settled Eira onto the floral sofa, wrapping a thick blanket around her shaking shoulders to ward off the chill from the open door they had finally managed to close.

Gabriel dragged a dining chair into the center of the living room, resting his acoustic guitar on his knee. The frontline defender was actively building his perimeter.

"If we're going to send a track out to the local stations, we can't just be 'Gabriel and Gwendolyn'," Gabriel pointed out, his brow furrowed in concentration. "We need a band name. Something solid. Something that commands attention on the dial."

Gwendolyn sat back down at the upright piano. Her eyes drifted to the window, where the December frost clung to the glass panes, illuminated by the outdoor holiday lights their mother had strung up just a few days ago.

"Think about Julian," Gwendolyn said softly, her tactical mind weaving the emotional core into the logistics. "What is his absolute favorite thing in the world? It's right now. It's the winter holidays. He loves the festive lights, he loves the decorations, the predictability of it... it's the only time of year he feels completely magical and safe."

She looked at Gabriel, her eyes catching the ambient light of the living room. "The Winter Palace."

Gabriel let the name sit in the air. It was perfect. It sounded massive, structural, and fiercely protective. It was an auditory castle, built entirely out of strings and chords, designed to shield a fourteen-year-old boy who couldn't protect himself.

"The Winter Palace," Gabriel confirmed, a heavy, definitive nod sealing the pact. "Okay. You said we start soft. We start with his baseline to keep him from panicking in the back of Tom's car."

"Wait," Gwendolyn said, suddenly standing up. She hurried down the hall to Julian's bedroom. A moment later, she returned carrying a small, plush, mechanical music box.

It was a relic from when Julian was a baby—a tactile toy he used to hold against his cheek when his unmedicated muscles spasmed and he needed to ground his sensory inputs. Gwendolyn sat back at the piano and turned the small metal key on the back of the toy.

The delicate, metallic tines plucked out a slow, fragile, incredibly familiar lullaby. To the rest of the world, it was the classic, immortal melody of Johannes Brahms's Wiegenlied. But to Gabriel and Gwendolyn, it didn't have a formal German title. It was simply Julian's song—the exact sequence of notes their family used to play to rock him to sleep when the world was too loud.

The pristine, tinkling notes filled the tense air of the living room. On the sofa, Eira let out a quiet, shuddering breath, closing her eyes as the familiar melody washed over her, grounding her shattered nerves.

"That's the intro," Gabriel said, his voice thick with emotion. He watched the small gears turn inside the music box. "We record that exactly as it is. It's the map in the dark."

Gwendolyn hovered her fingers over the high keys of the piano, perfectly matching the fragile pitch of the mechanical toy.

"And then," Gabriel growled, his hand gripping the neck of his acoustic guitar with bone-crushing force, "we bring the storm."

***

#### 8:45 AM • Gabriel Rowlands — The Garrison

Gabriel watched his twin's fingers ghost over the keys. The fragile, mechanical melody of the wind-up toy spun down, its tiny brass gears slowing to a halt. Gwendolyn instantly picked up the exact pitch on the upright piano, letting the pristine, fragile notes hang in the freezing air of the living room.

It was beautiful, but it was entirely too quiet. The silence was exactly where Julian's panic lived. Gabriel gripped the neck of his acoustic guitar, his knuckles turning white. He needed to shatter that silence.

"Okay," Gabriel muttered, his voice a low, gravelly rumble that vibrated in his chest. "The music box plays. It gives him his baseline. It tells him we're here. But Tom is driving. The road is loud. We need to cut through the engine noise and the tires on the asphalt."

Gwendolyn nodded, her sharp eyes focused on the board. Her left hand dropped to the lowest register of the piano. She struck a heavy, driving progression, the dark, minor chords echoing off the drywall.

"I'll arrange this for a cello," Gwendolyn commanded, her tactical mind building the architecture of the track in real-time. "Something dark and relentless. A driving string section. Like a heartbeat he can feel in his chest."

Gabriel felt the rhythm lock into his own pulse. He brought his calloused fingers down on the steel strings of his acoustic, striking a concussive, aggressive beat. He closed his eyes, imagining the worn acoustic was his solid-body electric guitar, plugged directly into a towering stack of overdriven amplifiers.

The lyrics bled out of him, raw and entirely unpolished, pulled directly from the freezing frost clinging to the living room windowpane.

"The clock upon the mantel chimes," Gabriel sang, his deep, dramatic baritone filling the space, carrying a terrifying weight of grief. "Reminding me of better times."

Gwendolyn seamlessly wove her voice into his, her vocal harmony acting as the perfect, haunting counterpoint as her fingers danced between the heavy bass notes and the delicate upper melody.

"The window pane is frosted white... I'm searching for a sign tonight."

On the floral sofa, Eira pulled the heavy blanket tighter around her shaking shoulders. She lifted her head, her red, tear-stained eyes watching her children. The sheer, desperate power in their voices was a physical force in the room. They weren't just singing; they were building an auditory fortress to protect a boy who couldn't protect himself.

"The pre-chorus," Gwendolyn directed, her voice sharp as she kept the tempo. "The strings need to build the tension here. We wind it up tight, like a bowstring."

Gabriel struck the chords harder, his pick hand moving in a rapid, aggressive blur. "Can you hear me calling? Can you see the glow? Through the miles of silence... and the drifting snow?"

"And then the drop," Gabriel said, his chest heaving as he abruptly slapped his hand flat against the strings, killing the sound. The sudden absence of music was jarring.

He looked down at his acoustic guitar. It just wasn't enough. The hollow wooden body couldn't hold the absolute, monolithic rage he needed to unleash.

"It hits on the word 'Candle'," Gabriel paced a tight circle on the carpet, the protective, frontline energy roaring back to life. "But Gwen... this isn't going to work with just the two of us. Not if we want to reach him."

Gwendolyn let her hands rest heavily on the keys. "It's too thin," she agreed, her analytical brain immediately recognizing the deficit. "An acoustic guitar and an upright piano won't rattle the floorboards of a car. It won't dominate a radio broadcast. We need the concussive kick. We need the heavy toms, the cymbals, and the distortion. We need it to sound massive."

They were aiming for a symphonic rock epic—a towering, theatrical wall of sound that blended classical orchestration with the raw, bleeding power of heavy metal. They needed the rest of the garrison.

Gabriel set his guitar down on the dining chair. He walked over to the kitchen wall phone, grabbing the same coiled receiver their mother had clung to just an hour ago.

"I'm calling Marcus," Gabriel decided, his jaw setting into absolute stone. "And Sarah. She still has her electric cello from the summer gigs, and Marcus has his full drum kit set up in his parents' garage."

Gwendolyn stood up from the piano bench, her eyes burning with unyielding resolve. "Tell them to grab their gear and get over here now. Tell them Julian is gone."

Gabriel punched the numbers into the keypad with rigid, determined strikes. They weren't just two broke eighteen-year-olds from James City County anymore, waiting helplessly for the police to file paperwork. They were The Winter Palace, and they were mobilizing for war.

## The War Room

### Part 1: The Reinforcements & The Makeshift Orchestra

**Date:** 1996-12-11 at 09:30 America/New_York

#### 9:30 AM • Gwendolyn Rowlands — The Reinforcements

The gravel driveway of the Cross property crunched loudly under the heavy, worn tires of a battered transit van.

Gabriel stood by the frosted front window, his jaw clenched, watching the vehicle throw the transmission into park. The doors swung open before the engine even fully cut out.

Marcus stepped out of the driver’s side, the collar of his denim jacket popped against the biting Virginia wind. He hauled a massive, heavy-duty canvas bag filled with steel drum hardware over his shoulder. From the passenger side, Sarah emerged. She didn't have her usual bright, easygoing smile; her face was set in a hard, serious line as she hoisted her thick, padded electric cello case onto her back like a shield.

They didn't bother knocking. They pushed through the front door, bringing a gust of freezing air with them into the tense, suffocating atmosphere of the living room.

The moment they crossed the threshold, the sheer gravity of the situation hit them. They saw Eira huddled on the floral sofa, a heavy blanket wrapped tightly around her shaking shoulders. And then, their eyes snapped to the corner of the room.

The empty space. The missing manual wheelchair.

"Gabriel," Marcus said, his voice dropping into a low, completely stunned register as he dropped his hardware bag onto the carpet with a heavy metallic clank. "Man... what happened? Where's Julian?"

"Tom," Gabriel stated, the name sounding like a curse in the quiet room. He didn't sugarcoat it, and he didn't use neurotypical filler to soften the blow. "He ambushed Mom at the door at eight o'clock this morning. He grabbed Julian right out of his seat, threw him in his car, and threw the chair in the trunk. He took him."

Sarah’s breath hitched. Her hands flew to her mouth in absolute horror. She knew exactly what that meant. Julian wasn't just missing; he was entirely unmoored.

"He doesn't have his weighted blanket," Sarah whispered, her eyes wide as she looked at Gwendolyn, who was standing rigidly by the piano. "He doesn't have his routine. He's trapped in a moving car with a man who doesn't know how to calm his sensory spikes."

"We called the police," Gwendolyn explained, her tactical mind stepping forward to manage the briefing. "They put out an alert on the interstate. But they are treating it as a domestic custody dispute while they process the paperwork. They aren't treating it as an immediate medical emergency. We are losing hours."

"So we aren't waiting for them," Gabriel growled, his fierce, protective energy radiating off him in waves. He looked at his two bandmates—his friends, his garrison. "We wrote a track. A beacon. If we can get it recorded, mixed, and rushed to the local radio stations, Julian might hear it. We need it to be loud. We need it to be massive."

Marcus didn't offer empty platitudes. He didn't tell them everything was going to be okay, and he didn't question the logic of trying to fight a kidnapping with a rock song. He understood the absolute, primal desperation of an older brother backed into a corner.

Marcus simply nodded, his expression hardening into stone. He reached down and grabbed the handles of his hardware bag.

"Then let's go to war," Marcus said firmly. "Where are we setting up?"

"The spare room," Gwendolyn directed, pointing down the narrow hallway. "We need to soundproof it as much as we can. We are going to push the limits of every amplifier we have."

They moved as a synchronized, completely focused unit. For the next thirty minutes, the hallway was a blur of frantic, heavy lifting. They carried the snare, the heavy floor toms, the kick drum, and the cymbals into the small, carpeted spare room.

Sarah unzipped her padded case, pulling out her sleek, solid-body electric cello. She plugged the heavy quarter-inch cable directly into an oversized bass amplifier, bypassing the delicate, classical acoustic tones for something capable of tearing the paint off the walls.

Gwendolyn dragged her heavy synthesizer keyboard into the corner, booting up the digital strings and choral patches, while Gabriel hauled his heavy amplifier and his solid-body electric guitar into the center of the cramped space.

The room was suffocatingly tight, smelling of hot dust from the warming amplifiers and the frantic sweat of sheer panic. Wires snaked across the floor like a chaotic nervous system.

Gabriel strapped his electric guitar across his chest. He turned the distortion knob on his amplifier all the way up, completely bypassing any attempt at restraint. He looked around the tiny, crowded room at Marcus, Sarah, and Gwendolyn.

"The band is called The Winter Palace," Gabriel announced over the low, vibrating hum of the live amplifiers. "The track is called Upon This Winter's Night. Gwen is going to start us off with his music box melody to establish the baseline. But when I give the signal... I want you to hit it so hard the foundation shakes."

Sarah tightened her grip on her cello bow, her eyes blazing with unwavering solidarity. Marcus settled onto his drum throne, raising his heavy wooden sticks.

The garrison was armed. The recording light clicked to a bright, violent red.

***

#### 9:45 AM • Gabriel Rowlands — The Wall of Sound

The spare room was suffocatingly hot, smelling of ozone, dust, and raw, unfiltered panic.

Gabriel stood in the center of the tangled mess of heavy black cables, his solid-body electric guitar strapped tight across his chest. He adjusted the heavy metal stand of the dynamic microphone, pulling it close to his mouth. Across the cramped room, Gwendolyn gave him a sharp, confirming nod from behind her synthesizer.

"Roll it," Gabriel commanded, his voice a low, gravelly rasp.

Marcus hit the record button on the archaic, four-track tape machine they had scavenged from a local pawn shop.

Gwendolyn’s fingers dropped onto the keys, replicating the fragile, pristine melody of Julian's music box. It was the anchor—the sound of their home, designed specifically to reach a boy who was autistic, non-verbal, and currently trapped in a sensory nightmare.

Gabriel closed his eyes. He pictured Julian in the back of Tom's car, miles away on I-64, terrified, freezing, and entirely without his perimeter. Gabriel’s chest heaved. He brought his pick down hard against the steel strings of his guitar, dialing the distortion up to a violent, jagged roar just as Marcus slammed his heavy boot onto the kick drum.

Sarah leaned into her electric cello, dragging her bow across the lowest string with punishing force. It wasn't classical music; it was a heavy, driving riff that hit Gabriel right in the sternum. It was the dark, relentless foundation of a massive, theatrical piece of symphonic rock.

Gabriel leaned into the microphone. He didn't just sing the lyrics; he weaponized them.

"The clock upon the mantel chimes... Reminding me of better times..."

He poured every ounce of his protective, older-brother fury into the vocal. He was the frontline shield, and right now, his voice was the only perimeter he had left to throw over his brother.

"The window pane is frosted white... I'm searching for a sign tonight."

They slammed into the pre-chorus. The strings built the tension, winding tighter and tighter. And then, they hit the drop on the word Candle. The small spare room physically shook from the sheer volume of the amplifiers.

But as they pushed through the chorus and reached the bridge, Gabriel abruptly slapped his palm flat against his guitar strings, killing the sound in a screech of harsh feedback.

Marcus stopped playing, his drumsticks hovering in the air. Sarah lowered her bow, breathing heavily.

"It's not enough," Gabriel growled, running a frustrated, trembling hand through his hair. He looked at the four of them crammed into the tiny space. "It's loud, but it's not massive. It sounds like four teenagers in a bedroom. If this is going to be a beacon, it needs to sound like an army. It needs to command the radio dial."

Gwendolyn stared at her synthesizer, her tactical mind instantly processing the deficit. "The bridge," she said, her voice sharp with realization. "When you sing 'I'm sending out a prayer', my voice isn't enough to answer you. We wrote it for a haunting choir. And we need a glockenspiel to pierce through the heavy bass tones."

"We need an orchestra," Sarah agreed, wiping sweat from her forehead. "We need it to sound like a rock opera."

Gabriel unstrapped his guitar, his jaw setting into absolute stone. The desperation was clawing at his throat, but he channeled it entirely into logistics. The 1996 single wasn't just a song anymore; it was a desperate, musical plea for his brother's life.

"I'm going back to the wall phone," Gabriel announced, turning toward the hallway. He looked at the drummer. "Marcus. Richard Harrison's girls. We need them here now."

"Maria and Clara," Marcus nodded instantly, catching Gabriel's wavelength. "Maria has the brass glockenspiel from the marching band. And Clara has perfect pitch. She basically runs the vocal arrangements for the youth choir."

To anyone else at the high school, the fourteen-year-old identical twins were just Richard Harrison's daughters. But Gabriel knew exactly what they were to his brother. They were Julian's absolute best friends.

Maria was the fierce, physical shield. Clara was the flawless auditory anchor. Because they looked and sounded exactly alike, down to the same dusting of freckles, Julian's easily overwhelmed brain registered them as a perfectly unified constant. Outside of Gwendolyn, Maria and Clara were the only girls in the world allowed into Julian's physical space. When Julian was overloaded, his absolute favorite safe harbor was being sandwiched between the twins on the sofa, anchored by a symmetrical, dual-sided deep-pressure cuddle.

If Julian was out there in the dark, he needed to hear his best friends calling him home.

"Call them," Gabriel ordered, stepping out into the hallway. "Call Richard at his office. Call the high school and get Maria and Clara on the line. I don't care if they're sitting in third period right now. Tell them to walk out. Tell them Julep was taken, and we need every single voice and instrument they can fit into this house."

Gwendolyn stood up from her keyboard, a fierce, desperate fire burning in her eyes.

"Tell them to park on the lawn if they have to," Gwendolyn added, her voice echoing down the hall. "We are going to build a wall of sound so thick that Tom won't be able to turn the dial without hearing us coming."

Gabriel grabbed the plastic receiver of the kitchen phone. He was eighteen, broke, and absolutely terrified. But as he began dialing numbers, preparing to turn their quiet Virginia home into a chaotic, crowded war room, he knew one thing for certain: the Harrison twins were going to drop everything and tear the world apart to save their best friend, and Gabriel was going to get his brother back, no matter how loud he had to scream to do it.

***

#### 10:15 AM • Gwendolyn Rowlands — The Makeshift Orchestra

Gabriel stood by the frosted front window, his chest tight, watching the driveway. The quiet, residential street in Ewell was suddenly transforming into a chaotic staging ground.

A heavy, reliable station wagon pulled aggressively onto the frost-covered grass of the front lawn, entirely disregarding the driveway. Two battered sedans full of high school band students pulled up right behind it. Car doors slammed in rapid succession.

Richard Harrison stepped out of the station wagon, his expression carved from absolute stone. Right behind him were his fourteen-year-old identical twin daughters. Clara and Maria marched up the walkway in perfect synchronization, their faces flushed from the freezing wind and blazing with a terrifying, fiercely protective rage. Clara was already organizing the sheet music in her hands, while Maria carefully balanced the heavy wooden case holding her marching-band glockenspiel.

Gabriel pulled the front door open, waving them inside. The freezing Virginia wind swept in with them, but the sheer, focused energy they brought instantly warmed the house. They didn't ask questions, and there was no casual small talk. When Gabriel had called Richard's office, the former music therapy teacher hadn't hesitated; he had driven straight to the high school, signed his daughters out of third period, and mobilized the frontline.

"We're here," Richard announced, shedding his heavy coat and dropping it onto the floor. He looked at Gabriel, projecting the quiet, unyielding authority of a man ready to go to war for his students. "Tell us what you need."

"We need a wall of sound," Gabriel said, his voice a low, gravelly rumble. He pointed toward the cramped spare room down the hall, where distorted guitar feedback was already humming like a live wire. "We need Clara's choir for the bridge, and we need Maria's glockenspiel to pierce through the distortion. We are recording a beacon."

Before Richard could respond, a sharp, defeated sob echoed from the kitchen.

Gabriel turned. His mother, Eira, was hanging up the heavy plastic wall phone, her hand trembling so violently she missed the cradle twice before it finally clicked into place.

"Mom?" Gabriel asked, closing the distance between them in three long strides. "Was that the precinct? Did they find the car?"

Eira shook her head, burying her face in her hands. "They haven't even issued the alert yet, Gabriel."

"What?!" Gabriel roared, his protective energy violently flaring. "It's been over two hours! He's non-verbal and he doesn't have his chair! He is critically endangered!"

"It's the bureaucracy," Eira wept, her voice muffled against her palms. "Because Tom is his biological father, the dispatcher said it's technically a civil custody dispute until they can physically verify the court orders. The archives are at the county courthouse. Everything is on paper. A clerk has to pull the physical divorce decree, verify that Tom has zero visitation rights, and get a judge to sign off on the breach before they can legally flag Julian as a kidnapping victim in the state system."

"How long?" Gwendolyn demanded, stepping out of the spare room. Her tactical mind was instantly calculating the horrifying delay.

"They said it could take until mid-afternoon," Eira choked out. "Gabriel... Tom could have him across the state line by then."

Gabriel’s vision swam with red. Mid-afternoon. Julian would be trapped in the back of a car for hours, subjected to unpredictable sensory inputs, unable to shift his own body weight, completely terrified and wondering why his family hadn't come for him.

"Then we don't wait for the police," Gabriel snarled, turning back to the makeshift orchestra crowding his living room. The desperation in his chest hardened into absolute, unyielding steel. "We finish this track right now."

Clara stepped forward, looking exactly like her twin sister but carrying a completely different auditory command. She looked at the hastily scribbled lyrics Gwendolyn had handed her. "Gabriel, even if we record a masterpiece in the next hour... how are you getting it out there? Local radio stations have corporate playlists. They don't just take cassette tapes from teenagers off the street."

"I don't know," Gabriel admitted, his voice cracking with sheer frustration. "I'll break into the broadcast booth if I have to."

"You won't have to break in," Richard Harrison spoke up quietly from the center of the room.

Gabriel looked at him.

Richard stepped forward, his eyes locking onto Eira's devastated face before shifting to Gabriel. "I left teaching to work corporate for a broadcasting group," Richard explained, his voice calm, steady, and entirely absolute. "We own a massive block of FM rock stations across Virginia and down into the Carolinas. And I am the regional program director who controls the live feed."

Gwendolyn’s eyes widened, the tactical puzzle instantly snapping together.

"I know Julian," Richard continued, his voice thick with emotion. "I know how he communicates, and I know how much he relies on his routine. If you record this beacon, I will bypass the corporate playlist. I will push the track live over the airwaves across the entire state. If Tom Cross turns on a radio today, I will make sure this is the only thing he hears."

Gabriel’s heart hammered a frantic, heavy rhythm against his ribs. It wasn't just a desperate shot in the dark anymore. They had a direct, viable pipeline to the state's broadcast towers. And Eira looked at Richard, seeing the exact, unwavering shield her family so desperately needed.

"Set up the microphones," Gabriel commanded, turning to Gwendolyn and the twins. "We push the vocals in the living room, keep the heavy rhythm section in the spare room. Clara, I need your choir to sound like an absolute army answering my prayer on the bridge. Maria, the glockenspiel has to be perfect."

The identical twins shared a single, synchronized nod, their best friend's life entirely on the line.

The house immediately exploded into chaotic, focused motion. Microphones were dragged into the living room, cables snaking across the carpets.

Gabriel strapped his electric guitar back across his chest, the heavy weight of it feeling like armor. He looked at the sheet of lyrics, picturing the freezing, desolate miles of the interstate. He was going to sing so loudly that the sheer force of it would shatter the windows of his father's car.

### Part 2: The Prayer

**Date:** 1996-12-11 at 11:15 America/New_York

The spare room and the adjoining hallway were packed wall-to-wall. The air was thick with the heat of glowing amplifiers, frantic breath, and absolute, unified desperation.

Gabriel stood in the center of the living room, the dynamic microphone gripped tightly in his calloused hands. Down the hall, Marcus counted off the tempo with a heavy clack-clack-clack of his drumsticks.

They tore through the first verse and the chorus in a single, breathless take. Gabriel’s voice was a raw, bleeding instrument, carrying the weight of a brother who had failed to block the front door. Behind him, Sarah’s electric cello carved a dark, relentless groove, while the brass section—crammed near the kitchen archway—swelled to build a massive, theatrical wall of sound.

"The bridge!" Gwendolyn yelled from her synthesizer, her fingers flying across the keys to shift the tempo into a driving, frantic rock-opera march. "Clara, go!"

Clara stepped up to the secondary microphone, leading the youth choir vocalists. They didn't just sing; they chanted with the perfect, flawless pitch of a marching army.

(Gabriel) "I’m sending out a prayer!" (Choir) "Bring him home! Bring him home!" (Gabriel) "To vanish all despair!" (Choir) "You are not alone!" In the center of the room, Gabriel closed his eyes. The tears he had been fighting all morning finally broke, tracking hot and fast down his cheeks, but his voice didn't waver. He leaned his entire body weight into the microphone stand.

"No matter where you are... no matter how far! Just look to the Northern Star!" Right on cue, Maria struck the metal keys of her glockenspiel. The bright, piercing, crystalline notes shattered through the heavy distortion of the guitars and the booming brass. It sounded exactly like stars bursting in the dark—a pristine, undeniable beacon cutting through the noise.

Gabriel ripped into his guitar solo, his fingers flying across the fretboard in a soaring, wailing plea. It was loud enough to rattle the picture frames in the hallway. It was loud enough to shake the foundation of the house.

As the final, massive orchestral hit echoed through the drywall, the heavy instruments abruptly cut out.

Gwendolyn let her hands drop to the lowest volume on the synthesizer, mimicking the delicate, fragile plinking of Julian’s wind-up toy. The track ended exactly how it began: with the quiet, safe, familiar lullaby of their home, fading slowly into total silence.

For five seconds, nobody in the house breathed.

Down the hall, Marcus hit the heavy, mechanical STOP button on the four-track tape machine. With a loud clack, he popped the plastic cassette tape out of the deck. He walked into the living room and pressed the warm plastic cassette directly into Gabriel’s hand.

"It's rough, and it's bleeding," Marcus said, his chest heaving. "But it's done."

Gabriel looked down at the tape. It wasn't just a song. It was a lifeline.

But as the adrenaline in the room finally crashed, the horrifying reality of the silence rushed back in. Clara dropped her sheet music. Her hands began to shake, and a sharp, ragged sob tore from her throat.

"He's overloading," Clara wept, the flawless control of her voice completely shattering as she looked at her twin sister and Gwendolyn. The agonizing intuition of a best friend was tearing her apart. "It's been hours. He's trapped in a moving car with that monster, and he doesn't have his weighted blanket. He's terrified. He needs us, Maria. He needs the pressure. He needs us to cuddle him right now."

Maria abandoned her glockenspiel instantly, crossing the room and pulling Clara into a fierce, desperate hug. Gwendolyn stepped out from behind the synthesizer and wrapped her arms tightly around both of the identical twins—forming a protective, heavy, three-person embrace that was a cruel, empty echo of the exact sensory hold Julian desperately needed.

Gabriel’s jaw clenched. He looked at Richard Harrison, who was already picking up the kitchen wall phone.

Richard dialed the direct line to his broadcasting control room. He didn't ask for permission; he pulled rank. "This is Harrison," Richard ordered his staff, his voice echoing with corporate, unyielding authority. "Pull the mid-day programming on the flagship rock station immediately. I have a priority track coming in. Clear the airwaves and keep the channel open."

Richard hung up the phone and pointed a sharp finger at Gabriel. "The board is yours. Get there as fast as you can."

Gabriel shoved the cassette tape into the pocket of his denim jacket. He grabbed his car keys off the kitchen counter.

"Gwen, stay here with Mom and the girls," Gabriel ordered, his frontline protector instincts shifting from the microphone to the asphalt. "If the police finally issue the alert, or if they find the car, I need to know immediately."

"Go," Gwendolyn urged, her arms still wrapped securely around the sobbing twins. "Get him on the air, Gabriel."

Gabriel bolted out the front door, the freezing December wind biting at his face. Marcus was right behind him. They sprinted across the frost-covered lawn to Gabriel's battered sedan, throwing themselves into the freezing interior. The engine roared to life, the tires spinning on the frosted driveway before catching traction.

Gabriel slammed the car into drive and tore out of the neighborhood, heading straight for the regional broadcast towers.

He gripped the steering wheel, his knuckles white. The cassette tape burned like a hot coal in his pocket. He didn't care about FCC regulations, corporate playlists, or broadcast fidelity. He just knew that his fourteen-year-old brother was trapped in a moving car somewhere out in the freezing expanse of the state, terrified, overstimulated, and desperately waiting for his family.

Turn on the radio, Tom, Gabriel prayed silently, his foot pressing harder on the accelerator. Turn on the radio, and let him hear us coming.

## The Signal

### Part 1: The Override & The Near Miss

**Date:** 1996-12-11 at 11:45 America/New_York

#### 11:45 AM • Richard Harrison — The Override

Gabriel’s battered sedan slid violently into the VIP parking lot of the regional broadcasting hub, the tires protesting sharply against the frozen asphalt.

Richard Harrison was out of the passenger seat before the car even came to a complete stop, his corporate security badge already in his hand. Gabriel and Marcus bailed out a second later, sprinting across the frost-covered pavement to keep pace with the towering, fiercely determined father.

Inside the lobby, the atmosphere was entirely disconnected from the raw, bleeding terror of the Cross household. It was quiet, climate-controlled, and hummed with the low, mundane energy of administrative work.

Richard didn't even break his stride. He blew right past the bewildered receptionist, swiping his heavy plastic badge against the scanner of the reinforced glass security doors. He didn't look like a corporate program director right now. With the sleeves of his dress shirt rolled up to his elbows and his jaw set in absolute stone, he looked exactly like the fiercely empathetic music therapy teacher he used to be—the man who had spent hours sitting on the floor with a much younger Julian, patiently using rhythm and vibration to help a non-verbal boy regulate his sensory overloads.

"Give me the tape," Richard ordered as the heavy doors buzzed open, leading them down a long, carpeted hallway.

Gabriel shoved his hand into his denim jacket and pulled out the plastic cassette. His hand was shaking so badly he nearly dropped it.

"It's rough," Gabriel rasped, his chest heaving as he pressed the tape into Richard's palm while they practically jogged down the corridor. "We just recorded it in the spare room. But it has his music box melody at the start. You know the one. We need him to hear it."

Richard stared down at the cassette, a fierce, protective recognition flashing in his eyes. He knew exactly what that melody meant to Julian. It was the ultimate anchor.

Richard pushed open a set of heavy, soundproof double doors, leading Gabriel and Marcus directly into Master Control. It was a massive, dimly lit room dominated by a sprawling analog mixing console, racks of heavy audio processors, and a wall of monitors displaying the live feeds of a dozen different FM stations.

The lone board operator on duty spun around in his chair, his eyes going wide at the sudden intrusion. "Mr. Harrison? Sir, you're not scheduled—"

"Step away from the board, Dave," Richard commanded, his voice echoing with unyielding corporate authority. He moved the operator aside and immediately took the captain's chair, his hands moving with surgical precision across the mixing board. He slammed a heavy plastic cartridge adapter into the primary deck and slid Gabriel's cassette inside.

"We don't know what direction he took," Gabriel explained frantically, the frontline energy surging as he looked at the massive geographical map mounted on the wall. "My mom thinks he got on Interstate 64 West. But it's been nearly four hours. He could be anywhere."

"Then we blanket the grid," Richard said, his fingers flying across a row of glowing toggle switches. "I manage the coastal syndicate, but that's not going to be enough if Tom is running for the interior. I'm patching in the central Virginia repeaters. And just to be safe, I'm pulling the override codes for our towers in the Shenandoah Valley and Winchester."

Marcus’s eyes widened. "Winchester? That's way out there."

"Winchester sits right at the top of the valley," Richard confirmed, his eyes locked on the audio meters. "That tower's signal bleeds directly through the mountains and pushes deep across the state line into West Virginia. If Tom is trying to lose himself in the backwoods, that signal will reach him. We are going to hijack the frequency of every major rock and contemporary station on this network."

Gabriel stared at the glowing red lights on the console. It was a staggering abuse of corporate power, a massive FCC violation, and the most beautiful thing he had ever seen.

Richard pulled a heavy studio microphone attached to an articulating arm and swung it directly in front of Gabriel.

"I'm killing the syndicated broadcast in exactly ten seconds," Richard warned, his hand hovering over the master fader. "I'm going to open your mic. You have fifteen seconds to speak directly to Julian before I trigger the playback. Tell him what he needs to hear, Gabriel. Be his shield."

Gabriel gripped the heavy metal base of the microphone. His heart hammered a violent, bruising rhythm against his ribs. He closed his eyes, visualizing Julian. He pictured the terrified fourteen-year-old trapped in a freezing car, overwhelmed by the sensory noise of the highway, desperately needing his older brother to block the doorway.

"Three," Richard counted down. "Two. One. You're live."

A sharp, electric hum crackled through the monitors as thousands of radios across the state were suddenly plunged into dead air.

Gabriel leaned into the microphone, his deep baritone completely stripped of its tough rock-frontman armor. It was just the raw, bleeding voice of a boy terrified for his family.

"Julian," Gabriel spoke, his voice trembling but projecting with absolute clarity. "It's Gabriel. Mom is safe. Gwen is safe. I know it's loud right now, and I know you're scared. But you need to listen to me. We are looking for you. We are not going to stop. I need you to listen to this song, Julian. Close your eyes, and hold onto it."

Gabriel gave Richard a sharp, definitive nod.

Richard slammed the playback button.

Over the massive studio monitors—and simultaneously out of hundreds of thousands of car speakers across the coastal flatlands, the central piedmont, and the freezing, jagged peaks of the Blue Ridge Mountains—the pristine, fragile, metallic plinking of Julian's music box echoed into the world.

It played for ten seconds, establishing the quiet, perfect safety of the Rowlands household.

And then, Gabriel’s heavy, distorted guitar and Sarah’s dark, driving cello detonated over the airwaves.

Upon This Winter's Night had officially been unleashed.

***

#### 12:04 PM • Julian Rowlands — Lost City

The heater in the stolen sedan was broken, blowing a pathetic, lukewarm draft against the freezing vinyl of the back seat.

Julian sat completely rigid, his knees pulled tightly to his chest. He was wearing only the thin, long-sleeved shirt he had been wearing at the breakfast table. He didn't have his heavy winter coat, and he didn't have the weighted sensory blanket that kept his nervous system grounded.

But worse than the cold was the absolute, terrifying loss of his physical autonomy.

Without his customized manual wheelchair, Julian was entirely unmoored. The heavy, unpredictable sway of the speeding car threw his body weight from side to side. His Cerebral Palsy made it impossible for him to right himself or brace against the sharp turns of the winding mountain roads. Every time the car jerked, his muscles locked in a painful, involuntary spasm.

He couldn't shift. He couldn't speak to ask for help. He was trapped in a terrifying, moving cage.

In the driver's seat, Tom kept his eyes glued to the desolate, winding asphalt of the highway. He hadn't spoken a single word to Julian since shoving him violently into the car four hours ago. There was no fatherly warmth, no explanation. Tom’s jaw was locked in a bitter, paranoid scowl, his eyes constantly darting to the rearview mirror to check for flashing police lights.

Through the frost-lined passenger window, Julian watched the towering, jagged peaks of the mountains swallow the horizon. The landscape was entirely alien. There were no flat coastal roads, no familiar marshlands, and no comforting pulse of the neighborhood holiday lights.

The car blew past a large, reflective green sign on the side of the highway.

WELCOME TO WEST VIRGINIA.

They had crossed the state line into the town of Lost City. The name was a perfect, devastating mirror of Julian's reality. He was completely, utterly lost.

A sharp, panicked whine built in the back of Julian's throat. The sensory overload of the rattling car and the sheer terror of isolation was pushing his autistic brain toward a massive, silent meltdown. His hands balled into tight fists, his unmedicated muscles trembling violently.

Annoyed by the sudden, distressed vocalization from the back seat, Tom let out a harsh, aggravated sigh.

"Keep it down back there," Tom snapped, his voice dripping with impatience.

To drown out the sound of Julian's panic, Tom reached forward and violently twisted the dial on the car's dashboard radio. He didn't care what station it landed on; he just needed noise to fill the suffocating silence of the getaway car.

Static hissed through the cheap car speakers, followed by the mid-day broadcast of a regional rock station.

Julian squeezed his eyes shut, his breathing turning rapid and shallow, completely unaware that his older brother was currently speeding down a Virginia highway with a cassette tape that was about to hijack the very airwaves currently filling the car.

***

#### 12:15 PM • Eira Rowlands — The Near Miss

The glowing red light on the mixing console clicked off. The broadcast was out in the wild, bleeding across the Virginia state lines, carried by invisible radio waves into the freezing mountain air.

Gabriel let go of the heavy studio microphone. His hands were shaking, the adrenaline that had fueled his frantic drive to the station suddenly crashing into a hollow, agonizing void. He looked up at Mr. Harrison, whose face was a mask of grim solidarity.

"Use the desk line," Mr. Harrison offered quietly, pushing a heavy, multi-button phone across the console. "Call your mother. Let her know the beacon is lit."

Gabriel snatched the receiver and dialed the familiar number with stiff, clumsy fingers. It rang exactly half a time before the line clicked open.

"Gabriel?" Eira’s voice was breathless, frantic, and strung tight with a terrifying anticipation.

"It's me, Mom," Gabriel said, leaning his heavy frame against the edge of the mixing board. "We did it. Mr. Harrison pushed it to the repeaters. It's playing right now across the whole grid. If Tom has the radio on, Julian will hear the music box."

"Gabriel, hold on," Eira gasped, the sound of a sharp, electronic beep echoing over the line. "That's call waiting. It's the county precinct. Don't hang up!"

There was a sharp click, followed by the agonizing, hollow static of being placed on hold.

Gabriel stopped breathing. He gripped the plastic receiver so tightly the casing groaned. Marcus stepped closer, his eyes wide, reading the sudden, absolute stillness in his friend's posture. Across the room, Mr. Harrison paused his work, the entire control room holding its breath.

They found him, Gabriel thought, a massive, desperate spark of hope igniting in his chest. The highway patrol found the car. It’s over.

Two full minutes passed. It felt like an entire lifetime.

Then, the line clicked back over.

It wasn't a sigh of relief. It was a broken, devastated, guttural sob.

"Mom?" Gabriel demanded, the hope in his chest instantly turning to ice. "Mom, what did they say? Did they find the car?"

"They had him," Eira wept, her voice collapsing into absolute hysterics. "Gabriel, they were looking right at him, and they let him drive away!"

"What are you talking about?!" Gabriel roared, his frontline protector instincts violently surging. "How do you let a kidnapper drive away?!"

"He crossed the state line," Eira choked out, struggling to pull air into her lungs. "A state trooper pulled Tom over in a town called Lost City, out in West Virginia. He was doing eighty-five in a fifty-five zone. The trooper walked right up to the window. He saw Julian in the back seat."

Gabriel’s vision swam. A police officer had been standing inches away from his brother.

"Julian is non-verbal!" Gabriel yelled into the receiver, his voice cracking with sheer, unadulterated rage. "He was probably spasming! He had to be terrified! How did the cop not see that he was in danger?!"

"Because Tom lied!" Eira screamed, her heartbreak echoing through the sterile broadcast booth. "Tom told the trooper they were just on a father-son road trip, and that Julian was just having a bad day with his disability! And because it's a different state... the trooper ran the license plates, Gabriel. He ran the plates, and nothing came up."

The geographical and bureaucratic reality hit Gabriel with the force of a physical blow.

"The paperwork," Gabriel whispered, the blood draining completely from his face.

"The clerk in James City County hasn't pushed the custody breach to the national database yet," Eira confirmed, sobbing uncontrollably. "To that state trooper, the car wasn't flagged in the NCIC system. There was no federal BOLO. It was just a speeding ticket. He wrote Tom a citation, and he let him merge back onto the highway."

Gabriel stared blankly at the acoustic foam on the studio wall. The system had failed. The absolute, sluggish incompetence of the bureaucracy had given his father a free pass. Tom was in West Virginia, burying Julian deeper into the desolate, freezing interior of the Appalachian mountains.

"We are entirely on our own," Gabriel rasped, his voice dropping into a terrifyingly cold, hollow register.

"Gabriel, what do we do?" Eira begged, completely shattered.

"You aren't on your own," Detective Miller interrupted, stepping forward. His expression hardened into absolute, uncompromising authority. "The moment that citation hit the regional dispatch, it triggered the interstate net. Your father crossed a state line with a child he has absolutely no legal right to possess. This is no longer a local civil dispute. It is a federal kidnapping."

Gwendolyn’s breath hitched. The tactical shift was monumental.

"The FBI has officially been notified, and we have pushed a Critical Missing Child Bulletin to the NCIC database," the detective continued, laying out the invisible arsenal they were finally deploying. "Every highway patrol unit, every weigh station, and every toll booth from here to the Midwest has his license plate on their dashboard computers. If a single cop runs those tags, it’s going to light up like a Christmas tree. And because Julian is without his manual wheelchair and his required medication, he is officially classified as critically endangered."

Gabriel looked over at the mixing console, where the audio meters for Upon This Winter's Night were still bouncing in the red, projecting their desperate, symphonic war cry across the mountains.

"We pray he leaves the radio on," Gabriel said, his jaw setting into uncompromising stone. "I'm coming home, Mom."

He slammed the receiver down. The beacon was the only weapon they had left.

### Part 2: The Hijack & The Longest Shadow

**Date:** 1996-12-11 at 12:20 America/New_York

#### 12:20 PM • Julian Rowlands — The Hijack

The heavy tires of the sedan crunched over the frozen gravel of the shoulder, throwing a spray of dirty slush as Tom aggressively merged back onto the two-lane West Virginia highway.

He snatched the yellow paper citation from his lap and tossed it violently onto the dashboard. He let out a harsh, ragged breath, his heart hammering against his ribs. It had been entirely too close. The state trooper had shined his heavy flashlight right into the back seat. But Tom had played the part perfectly—the exhausted, patient father dealing with a disabled son having a "bad day."

Because the James City County clerk hadn't pushed the custody breach to the national wire yet, the computer in the trooper's cruiser had returned a completely clean record.

Tom gripped the steering wheel, his knuckles turning white as he accelerated back up to seventy miles an hour, pushing deeper into the isolating, jagged terrain of the Appalachian Mountains.

In the back seat, Julian sat completely rigid. His unmedicated muscles were locked into a terrifying, painful state of spasticity. The freezing draft from the broken heater, combined with the sheer, crushing terror of the traffic stop, was pushing his sensory system to the absolute brink. When the police officer had looked at him, Julian had tried to make a sound, to signal his distress, but his vocal cords had completely frozen in panic.

He was trapped. The coastal flatlands of his home were gone, replaced by towering, skeletal trees and walls of gray rock.

Irritated by the heavy, suffocating silence in the car and the quiet, strained hitching of Julian's breathing, Tom reached a hand out and twisted the volume knob on the dashboard radio.

The speakers crackled to life, fighting through a layer of mountain static. A polished, pre-recorded radio jingle echoed through the cabin.

“You’re locked into the Valley’s Rock... 97.3 Winchester, your Shenandoah connection.”

A generic grunge track started to play, the heavy bass vibrating through the floorboards. Tom settled back into his seat, the noise providing a distraction from the reality of what he had just done.

But less than thirty seconds later, the music abruptly, violently cut out.

It wasn't a fade. It was a hard, digital kill-switch. A sharp pulse of static hissed through the car speakers, followed immediately by five seconds of absolute dead air.

Tom frowned, reaching a hand out to smack the side of the plastic dashboard, assuming the mountain terrain had killed the signal.

Then, a voice pierced the silence. It didn't belong to a polished radio DJ. It was raw, breathless, and trembling with a terrifying, protective fury.

"Julian."

In the back seat, Julian violently gasped. His wide, terrified eyes snapped toward the front dashboard. His chest heaved.

"It's Gabriel," the voice crackled over the FM frequency, bouncing off the Winchester broadcast towers and bleeding directly into the freezing West Virginia air. "Mom is safe. Gwen is safe. I know it's loud right now, and I know you're scared. But you need to listen to me. We are looking for you. We are not going to stop. I need you to listen to this song, Julian. Close your eyes, and hold onto it."

Tom slammed his foot on the brake pedal, the sedan swerving slightly on the icy asphalt.

"What the hell?" Tom hissed, staring at the radio interface in absolute, uncomprehending shock.

A moment later, the pristine, metallic, delicate plinking of a wind-up toy began to play through the cheap car speakers.

It was Wiegenlied. It was the exact melody of the plush music box that sat on Julian’s nightstand in Ewell.

For Julian, the sound was an instant, physical tether. The crushing, suffocating terror of the mountains completely evaporated. The music box was the sound of safety. It was the sound of his brother sitting on the edge of his bed, holding the perimeter.

Julian let out a loud, breathless vocalization of pure recognition. He wasn't lost anymore. They knew where he was.

"The clock upon the mantel chimes..." Gabriel’s deep, dramatic baritone blasted through the speakers, immediately followed by the massive, concussive drop of a heavily distorted electric guitar and a driving, relentless cello.

"Reminding me of better times!"

"How?!" Tom roared, his face flushing a violent, panicked red. He reached out and violently twisted the tuning dial, trying to escape the auditory assault.

The frequency jumped from 97.3 to 98.1.

"...The window pane is frosted white!" Gabriel’s voice continued seamlessly on the new station.

Tom spun the dial again, pushing it to 101.5.

"...I'm searching for a sign tonight!"

Mr. Harrison’s corporate override was absolute. The coastal broadcasting group owned half the frequencies in the Shenandoah Valley, and every single one of them was currently transmitting the exact same desperate, symphonic war cry.

Julian’s hands uncurled. The rigid tension in his legs began to melt. He didn't cover his ears against the loud, distorted guitars, because the noise wasn't chaotic anymore. It was a massive, impenetrable wall of armor built entirely for him.

Tom slammed his fist against the radio, finally hitting the power button and killing the audio completely. The car plunged back into a suffocating, ringing silence.

Tom was breathing heavily, his eyes darting frantically to the rearview mirror. The delusion that he had gotten away with it cleanly was entirely dead. His eighteen-year-old son hadn't just filed a police report; he had hijacked the regional commercial airwaves.

And miles behind them, in a dispatch center across the Virginia state line, the agonizing bureaucratic delay was finally breaking. A county clerk hit Enter on a keyboard, linking the custody breach to the state database.

Tom didn't know it yet as he sped deeper into the West Virginia interior, but the speeding ticket in his lap was no longer his biggest problem. The Critical Missing Child Bulletin had just officially hit the NCIC wire, and the net was rapidly closing.

***

#### 1:15 PM • Gabriel Rowlands — The Escalation

Gabriel’s battered sedan slid into the driveway of the Ewell house, the engine ticking as he cut the ignition. He didn't move immediately. He just sat behind the steering wheel, his hands still gripping the worn plastic rim, listening to the car speakers.

Through the static, the haunting, massive choir of Upon This Winter's Night was echoing across the regional broadcast.

It was surreal. His own raw, desperate prayer was bleeding out of the dashboard, carried by invisible waves into the freezing December sky. In the backseat, Marcus let out a long, heavy exhale, staring at the radio. In the passenger seat, Richard Harrison sat in quiet, unyielding solidarity, the corporate program director having successfully torn up his own network’s rulebook.

"It's out there, Gabriel," Richard said softly, his voice carrying the steady, grounding weight of a father. "We did everything we could do."

Gabriel nodded, a sharp ache settling deep in his chest. He pulled the keys from the ignition, killing the audio, and the three of them stepped out into the biting wind.

When Gabriel pushed through the front door, the atmosphere inside the house had fundamentally shifted. The chaotic, frantic energy of the makeshift recording studio was gone. The instruments were abandoned in the spare room, the amplifiers cooling off.

In the living room, Eira was no longer collapsed on the floor. She was sitting rigidly on the edge of the floral sofa, her hands clasped tightly in her lap. Sitting shoulder-to-shoulder on either side of her were Maria and Clara, the identical twins flanking Julian's mother like a fiercely protective honor guard.

Standing across from them, wearing a heavy wool trench coat and holding a leather-bound notepad, was a county detective. Gwendolyn stood right behind the sofa, her arms crossed, her eyes locked onto the investigator with a cold, calculating intensity.

Gabriel’s heavy boots thudded against the hardwood, drawing the detective's attention.

"Gabriel," Gwendolyn said, her voice tight but remarkably steady. "This is Detective Miller. The county clerk finally processed the paperwork."

Gabriel stepped directly into the living room, his broad shoulders squared, instinctively placing himself between the detective and his family. Richard stepped up right beside him, reinforcing the perimeter.

"Did you find him? We know about the traffic stop," Gabriel demanded.

Detective Miller let out a slow, measured breath, closing his notepad. He looked at the eighteen-year-old boy, recognizing the absolute, volatile protectiveness radiating from him.

"We did," Detective Miller confirmed, his tone carrying the grave, heavy weight of a massive bureaucratic failure. "The state police in West Virginia forwarded the incident report a few minutes ago. The traffic stop occurred in a jurisdiction called Lost City."

Gabriel flinched. Lost City. The name hit his chest like a physical blow. It wasn't just a town; it was a devastating reflection of his brother's reality. Julian was entirely unmoored, dragged deep into the isolating, jagged terrain of the Appalachian Mountains. Clara let out a sharp, devastated breath, her hand instantly finding Maria's and gripping it tight.

"A state trooper pulled your father over for excessive speed," the detective explained, maintaining direct eye contact with Gabriel. "Because the custody breach hadn't hit the national wire yet, the plates came back clean. Your father claimed it was a standard family trip. The trooper wrote the citation and let them merge back onto the highway."

"He looked right at my brother," Gabriel snarled, his voice trembling with a dark, violent fury. "Julian is fourteen. He's completely non-verbal. He has Cerebral Palsy. His muscles lock up when he's terrified! How does a trained officer look at a boy in that condition and just let the car drive away?!"

"Gabriel," Eira whispered, her voice cracking as she reached out.

"He didn't have the context, son," Detective Miller said calmly, refusing to be baited into an argument. "Your father lied, and the system was too slow to catch him. But the system is fully online now."

The detective stepped forward, his expression hardening into absolute, uncompromising authority.

"The moment that citation hit the regional dispatch, it triggered the interstate net," Miller stated. "Your father crossed a state line with a child he has absolutely no legal right to possess. This is no longer a local civil dispute. It is a federal kidnapping."

Gwendolyn’s breath hitched. The tactical shift was monumental.

"We have officially issued a critical missing child bulletin across all neighboring states," the detective continued, laying out the arsenal they were finally deploying. "Every highway patrol unit, every weigh station, and every toll booth from here to the Ohio border has the license plate and the vehicle description. And because Julian is without his manual wheelchair and his required medication, the state police have officially classified him as critically endangered."

Critically endangered. The words echoed in the quiet living room. It was the absolute worst-case scenario, printed in black and white on a police report, but it was also the exact leverage they needed. The authorities were finally treating Julian's disappearance with the sheer, life-or-death urgency it demanded.

"He's in the mountains," Gabriel said, his voice dropping into a hollow, terrified rasp. "It's freezing out there. He doesn't have a coat. He doesn't have his weighted blanket to stop the sensory overloads."

"Which is why we are tearing the highway apart looking for him," Detective Miller promised. "We have units sweeping the major routes leading out of Lost City right now."

The detective offered a small, empathetic nod to Eira and Richard before turning toward the front door. "Stay by the phone. The second we have a visual on that vehicle, I will call you."

As the heavy front door clicked shut behind the investigator, the suffocating reality of the wait settled over the house.

Gabriel looked at Gwendolyn, and then down at Clara and Maria. They had mobilized an army. They had hijacked the regional radio towers. They had forced the federal authorities to launch a massive dragnet.

But as Gabriel sank into the dining chair, staring blindly out the frosted window toward the west, he knew that none of the police cruisers mattered right now.

Julian was alone in the freezing, jagged wilderness of West Virginia. And until the flashing lights found Tom's car, the only thing keeping his little brother tethered to the world was the sound of a fragile music box playing through the radio static.

***

#### 6:00 PM • Eira Rowlands — The Longest Shadow

By six o'clock, the winter sun had completely surrendered, plunging James City County into a bitter, freezing night.
