"Enough."

The single word wasn't shouted, but it cracked through the room with the devastating authority of a whip.

Mr. Evans was suddenly standing at the end of the aisle. The older substitute towered over Kevin's desk, his expression carved from absolute stone.

"But he's—" Kevin started to complain, pointing a finger at Julian.

"Gather your belongings," Mr. Evans interrupted, his voice deathly quiet and perfectly level. He pulled a disciplinary referral pad from his jacket pocket and began writing with rapid, ruthless precision. "You do not demand language from anyone in this room, nor do you mock a fellow student. You will take this referral to the principal's office immediately. If you speak another word before you cross that threshold, I will personally ensure your suspension lasts until the summer."

Kevin looked at the substitute's cold, unyielding eyes, swallowed hard, grabbed his backpack, and practically sprinted out of the classroom.

The heavy door clicked shut. The classroom was dead silent.

Clara and Maria were still standing defensively in front of Julian, their chests heaving. Julian was gripping the armrests of his manual chair, his knuckles entirely white, his breathing shallow and erratic. He waited for the inevitable. He waited for the strict disciplinarian to turn his wrath on the boy who had just disrupted the reading packet with a loud, electronic foreign language.

Mr. Evans tore the copy of the referral from his pad. He looked down at the identical twins, offering a slow, respectful nod to the fiercely protective girls.

Then, the substitute took a step closer to Julian's wheelchair. The severe, intimidating mask of the disciplinarian instantly melted away. Mr. Evans looked down at the spiral-bound notebook on Julian's desk. He saw the Welsh translations. He looked at the trembling boy, and then he looked at the AAC device.

In a single, brilliant moment of observation, the older man understood exactly what he was looking at. He recognized the heavy, suffocating weight of trauma. He knew why a boy would need to hide from the English language inside an English classroom.

Mr. Evans crouched down slowly, bringing himself down to Julian's eye level so he wasn't looming.

"<lang="cy" trans="It's alright, boy,">Mae'n iawn, fachgen,</lang>" Mr. Evans said softly.

Julian gasped, his eyes going wide. The words were perfectly accented, rolling with the familiar, deeply comforting cadence of his own home.

"<lang="cy" trans="You did nothing wrong,">Fe wnaethoch chi ddim byd o'i le,</lang>" the substitute continued in flawless Welsh, his tone carrying a profound, anchoring warmth. "<lang="cy" trans="I see your notes. It is okay to take refuge in your language. I understand.">Rwy'n gweld eich nodiadau. Mae'n iawn i gymryd lloches yn eich iaith. Rwy'n deall.</lang>"

The rigid tension locked in Julian's shoulders completely shattered. The classroom wasn't a threat anymore. The perimeter had been secured by an unexpected ally.

Julian dropped his hands from his armrests. He let out a long, ragged exhale, slumping back into his chair. He reached for his AAC device, keeping it firmly locked on the Welsh synthesizer.

<aac><lang="cy" trans="THANK YOU VERY MUCH.">DIOLCH YN FAWR.</lang></aac>

"<lang="cy" trans="You're welcome,">Croeso,</lang>" Mr. Evans smiled gently. He stood back up, adjusting his tweed jacket, and looked at Clara and Maria. "Take your seats, girls. You did well."

As the substitute walked calmly back to the front of the room to resume the lesson, Julian let himself relax, Clara’s hand coming to rest firmly on his left shoulder while Maria squeezed his right arm. He looked at the chalkboard, and then down at his Welsh notes. For the first time all semester, he didn't feel like he was surviving English class; he actually felt safe.

He tapped a quick, quiet message on his device and showed the screen to Clara and Maria.

<aac>I WISH HE WAS OUR REGULAR TEACHER.</aac>

Clara and Maria leaned in, identical, relieved smiles spreading across their faces.

"Me too, Julep. Me too," they whispered back in perfect unison.

***

#### 10:25 AM — The Mother Tongue

The heavy classroom door remained firmly shut, separating Kevin and his disciplinary referral from the rest of the freshman English class.

Inside Room 114, the remaining twenty-nine students sat in complete, stunned silence.

They had just witnessed the strictest substitute in the building obliterate the class bully, and then immediately drop to a crouch to softly comfort the quiet boy in the wheelchair using an unrecognizable, rhythmic language.

Mr. Evans walked slowly back to the chalkboard. He picked up a piece of white chalk and turned to face the room.

He could see the curiosity practically vibrating in the teenagers' eyes. They weren't hostile; they were completely captivated.

Mr. Evans looked over the rows of desks, his gaze landing gently on the back corner.

He offered a slight, respectful tilt of his head toward Julian, Maria, and Clara.

"Julian. Maria. Clara," Mr. Evans addressed them quietly, treating the fourteen-year-olds with the diplomatic respect of equals. "Ignorance breeds fear, and fear breeds cruelty. With your permission... may we demystify the language for them?"

Julian sat comfortably against the backrest of his manual chair, the lingering adrenaline of his near-meltdown entirely flushed from his system.

He looked at Maria and Clara, who flanked him on either side. The identical twins offered him small, synchronized, encouraging nods.

Julian pulled his AAC device forward, keeping the synthesizer locked to his safe harbor.

<aac><lang="cy" trans="YES. TEACH THEM.">OES. DYSGWCH NHW.</lang></aac>

The digitized Welsh echoed through the room. A few students in the front row leaned forward, completely fascinated by the metallic, rolling consonants.

Mr. Evans smiled warmly. He turned to the chalkboard and wrote a single word in sharp, precise cursive: Cymraeg.

"What you just heard," Mr. Evans announced, projecting his voice clearly across the room, "is Cymraeg. In English, it is known as Welsh. It is a Celtic language, spoken natively in the country of Wales, which lies to the west of England. It is one of the oldest living languages in Europe."

He tapped the chalk against the board. "To an untrained ear, it sounds alien. The alphabet is different. We use letters like 'W' and 'Y' as vowels. We roll our R's, and we use a sound—the double L—that does not exist in the English language."

The substitute wrote two basic phrases on the board: Bore da and Diolch.

"Bore da means 'Good morning,'" Mr. Evans instructed, pronouncing it clearly. "And Diolch means 'Thank you.' Go ahead. Try it."

A disjointed, thoroughly Americanized chorus of Bore da and Diolch echoed back to him. The class was entirely hooked.

The rigid, boring English reading packet was forgotten, replaced by the thrilling discovery of a secret, ancient code right in their own classroom.

Julian watched his classmates attempt the pronunciation. A small, genuine smile touched the corners of his mouth.

The language of his sanctuary wasn't being mocked anymore; it was being respected.

"Now," Mr. Evans continued, a distinct spark of national pride lighting up his stern features. "The Welsh are very proud of our heritage. In fact, we hold the title for the longest, most complicated town name in all of Europe. It is a badge of honor for any true Welshman to be able to pronounce it in a single breath."

He turned to the chalkboard, preparing to write the fifty-eight-letter behemoth across the slate. "The town is called—"

"Llanfairpwllgwyngyllgogerychwyrndrobwllllantysiliogogogoch," two voices chimed in perfect, flawless unison from the back row.

Mr. Evans froze, the chalk hovering an inch from the blackboard.

The entire class turned around in their seats.

Maria and Clara sat perfectly straight at their desks, flanking Julian's wheelchair. They hadn't stumbled. They hadn't paused for breath. They had rolled the complex, breathy ll consonants and the guttural ch with absolute, breathtaking, synchronized perfection.

They sounded exactly like Eira Rowlands in stereo.

Julian tapped his armrest rapidly, letting out a bright, highly vocalized laugh of pure delight.

Mr. Evans slowly lowered his piece of chalk. He stared at the fourteen-year-old American identical twins in absolute, unadulterated shock.

"I'm sorry... could you translate that for the class, girls?"

Maria and Clara turned to look at each other. Identical, slightly evil grins broke across their faces. They had been taught the translation by Eira years ago, and they didn't just know it by heart—they knew exactly how to weaponize their twin dynamic for maximum dramatic effect.

They turned back to the substitute teacher, ping-ponging the translation back and forth, one word at a time, without a single microsecond of hesitation.

"It," Maria started. "Translates," Clara continued. "To," Maria said. "Saint," Clara fired back. "Mary's," "Church," "in," "the," "hollow," "of," "the," "white," "hazel," "near," "to," "the," "rapid," "whirlpool," "of," "Llantysilio," "of," "the," "red," "cave."

Clara delivered the final word with a sharp, satisfied nod. The stereophonic performance was completely flawless and undeniably unnerving.

The classroom erupted into spontaneous, impressed applause. A boy near the windows let out a low whistle of disbelief.

The twins waited for the noise to die down, offering a casual, very American shrug that contrasted completely with their flawless Celtic ping-pong match. They had never stepped foot outside of Virginia—save for the terrifying drive to Ohio to bring Julian home—but they had spent seven years entirely immersed in the Rowlands family perimeter.

"But if you actually live there, nobody says the whole thing," Maria clarified, her standard American accent returning to match her sister's. "Julian's mom says it's mostly for the tourists and the train station sign. Locals just call it Llanfairpwll, or Llanfair PG if you're in a hurry."

Mr. Evans leaned against his heavy wooden desk, completely abandoning his strict, disciplinarian persona.

He let out a rich, genuine laugh, shaking his head in sheer amazement.

"Spot on, girls. Absolutely spot on," Mr. Evans praised, his eyes shining.

He looked specifically at the three teenagers in the back row.

"And as it happens... I know exactly what that rapid whirlpool looks like. My name is Jordan Evans, and I was born and raised two streets over from that very church."

Julian’s breath hitched. He looked at the substitute teacher, his eyes going incredibly wide.

Of all the substitute teachers in the entire James City County public school district. Of all the classrooms, on all the days. The universe had dropped a man from his exact home village right onto the frontline to protect him.

Julian pulled his hands off his armrests and quickly typed on his AAC device. He didn't use the English output, and he didn't need to. He was speaking directly to a man who understood his perimeter perfectly.

<aac><lang="cy" trans="I WAS BORN THERE TOO. WE MOVED TO AMERICA WHEN I WAS SIX. YOU ARE THE BEST TEACHER EVER.">CEFAIS FY NGENI YNO HEFYD. SYMUDON NI I AMERICA PAN OEDDWN I'N CHWECH. CHI YW YR ATHRO GORAU ERIOED.</lang></aac>

Jordan Evans read the translation from the cadence of the robotic voice.

He froze, his eyes widening slightly as the immense weight of that statement hit him.

This wasn't just a boy clinging to his mother's heritage. This was a native son of Anglesey. This was a boy who had spent the first six years of his life breathing the cold salt air of the Menai Strait before being uprooted and brought to America.

It made the boy's desperate, fierce attachment to the Welsh language instantly, infinitely more profound. It was his first home.

A profound, deeply moved smile softened the older man's strict features.

He placed his right hand over his heart and offered a slight, formal bow to the boy in the wheelchair.

"<lang="cy" trans="It is an honor to meet you, Julian,">Mae'n anrhydedd i cwrdd â chi, Julian,</lang>" Mr. Evans replied warmly.

He held Julian's gaze, his voice dropping to a register of absolute, validating safety.

"<lang="cy" trans="And remember... no matter how far you travel, you are always home in our language.">A chofiwch... waeth pa mor bell ydych chi'n teithio, rydych chi bob amser adref yn ein hiaith ni.</lang>"

Julian let out a long, shuddering sigh of absolute relief, his left hand finding Maria's and his right hand finding Clara's, holding onto his anchors tight.

The bell rang overhead, signaling the end of the period.

But as the freshman class packed up their bags, no one was talking about the English reading packet, and no one was talking about Kevin getting sent to the office.

They were all talking, and trying to pronounce, the incredible, ancient language that belonged to the quiet boy in the back row.

***

#### 2:45 PM — The Transit Loop

When the final bell of the day rang, James City County High School erupted into a chaotic, echoing bottleneck of slamming lockers and shouting teenagers.

Julian kept his head down, his heavy noise-isolating headphones firmly clamped over his ears. He navigated his manual wheelchair smoothly through the crowded corridor, perfectly synchronized with Maria, who walked flush against his left wheel. She acted as a physical bumper, expertly steering oblivious freshmen and rushing upperclassmen away from Julian's personal space.

They pushed through the heavy double doors and out into the crisp, bright afternoon air.

During Julian's chaotic freshman enrollment meeting back in January, the county’s transportation department had aggressively pushed for Julian to take the specialized special-needs bus. They had presented the smaller, yellow bus with the hydraulic lift as the safest, most logical option.

Eira Rowlands had taken one look at the district's strict liability policies—which stated that only legally disabled students and assigned aides could ride, strictly forbidding friends or siblings—and had flatly rejected it.

“If Maria is not on the manifest, Julian is not on the vehicle,” Eira had stated, her Welsh accent carrying absolute, zero-tolerance authority. “My son will take the city transit.”

And so, every afternoon at 2:45, Julian and Maria bypassed the loud, diesel-choked lineup of yellow school buses in the loading zone.

Instead, they walked to the edge of the campus, stopping at the crosswalk of Campus Drive. It was a quiet, tree-lined suburban street with a strict twenty-five-mile-per-hour speed limit, separating the high school from the sprawling brick buildings of Williamsburg Community College.

Maria looked both ways, her hand resting protectively on the back of Julian's chair, before they crossed the quiet asphalt.

The moment they pulled open the heavy glass doors of the WCC library, the sensory environment drastically shifted. The chaotic, immature screaming of the high school vanished, replaced by the hushed, mature ambient noise of college students quietly typing on laptops and flipping through massive textbooks. It smelled like old paper, floor wax, and the dark roast coffee from the campus café.

Julian pulled his headphones down to rest around his neck, letting out a long, quiet sigh of relief.

They navigated through the stacks of reference books, heading toward a large oak table in the quietest corner of the second floor.

Gabriel and Gwendolyn were already there.

Gabriel was slouched in a wooden chair, a pair of reading glasses perched on his nose as he highlighted a massive Sociology textbook. Gwendolyn sat across from him, her posture perfect, rapidly cross-referencing two different notebooks for her macroeconomics class.

When Gabriel saw the younger teens approaching, his face instantly softened. He closed his textbook, tossing his highlighter onto the table.

"<lang="cy" trans="How was it today, Julep?">Sut oedd hi heddiw, Julep?</lang>" Gabriel asked softly, his deep baritone keeping to the quiet, library-appropriate volume.

Julian pulled up to the end of the table. He didn't type on his AAC device; he just offered a small, exhausted, but genuine smile, flashing a quick thumbs-up.

"The sub in English Lit was from Anglesey," Maria whispered, pulling out a chair and dropping her heavy backpack onto the floor. "He totally put Kevin in his place, and then taught the whole class how to say Bore da."

Gwendolyn stopped writing, looking up with a sharp, incredibly impressed smirk. "A Welshman holding the line in English Lit? Gwych. (Brilliant.) We'll have to send him a thank-you note."

They stayed in the quiet sanctuary of the library for another forty-five minutes, allowing Julian's nervous system to completely decompress after the sensory load of the school day. He leaned his head against Maria's shoulder, watching his older siblings study, entirely at peace within the fully reassembled perimeter.

At 3:45 PM, Gwendolyn checked her watch and tapped the table. "Alright, garrison. Time to move."

The four teenagers packed up their bags and walked out of the library, heading toward the main PRT bus shelter at the edge of the college campus.

At exactly 3:52 PM, the heavy, high-floor transit bus pulled up to the curb. It wore the familiar, comforting tri-color livery of Peninsula Regional Transport—a crisp white roof, a thick dark maroon stripe, and a tan lower body.

Above the windshield, the mechanical flipdot sign cascaded with its rapid, rhythmic, incredibly soothing clack-clack-clack.

BLUE LINE

Clack-clack-clack.

TRANSPORTATION CENTER

The heavy pneumatic doors hissed open. The bus driver, a friendly older woman who knew their routine by heart, offered a warm smile.

Gabriel and Gwendolyn stepped up first, flashing their laminated WCC college IDs to the driver to cover the fare. Maria stepped up next, showing her Ewell High School ID.

"Lift's coming down, Julian!" the driver called out, hitting a heavy switch on the dashboard.

The mechanical whine of the wheelchair lift engaged, the heavy metal platform lowering smoothly to the curb. Julian backed his manual chair onto the lift, locking his brakes. With a hydraulic hum, he was hoisted aboard the high-floor bus.

Gabriel paid the fare for him, tapping Julian's high school ID against the farebox, while Maria guided the wheelchair into the securement bay, expertly snapping the heavy floor restraints into place.

Gabriel sat in the row directly behind Julian, while Gwendolyn sat across the aisle, scanning the rest of the passengers with her usual tactical vigilance. Maria sat in the flip-down jump seat directly beside Julian's wheel, their knees touching.

The air brakes hissed, and the heavy bus pulled away from the curb.

It was only a five-minute ride back to their neighborhood. It was a short, perfectly calibrated loop. But as Julian looked out the tinted window, feeling the heavy, grounding rumble of the diesel engine beneath his chair and the absolute certainty of his family surrounding him, he knew it was the best part of his day.

# Era 4: The Menai Strait (August 1998)

## Outbound

### Part 1: The I-64 Corridor & The Ascent

**Date:** 1998-08-02 at 10:00 America/New_York

#### 10:00 AM • Gwendolyn Rowlands — The I-64 Corridor

August in the Virginia tidewater is not just a season; it is a physical, suffocating weight. The morning air outside the Ewell house was thick with a sweltering, 90-degree humidity that made the horizon shimmer over the asphalt.

But inside the heavy, customized wheelchair van tearing westward up Interstate 64, the climate was absolutely, rigidly controlled.

The air conditioning roared from the dashboard vents, keeping the cabin incredibly cool. At fifteen years old, Julian had grown taller, his frame lengthening into a young man’s, but his reliance on the sensory perimeter of his family was just as absolute as it had been when he was a child. He sat securely locked into the floor restraints in the center of the van, wearing his dark navy Aran wool sweater—the exact one his mother had bought him for the quiet Réveillon of '96—despite the brutal Virginia heat outside. It wasn't about temperature; it was about the heavy, grounding weight of the tight stitches holding his nervous system together.

On his left, occupying the flip-down jump seat, sat fifteen-year-old Clara. On his right sat her identical twin, Maria. Their knees were pressed flush against the thick rubber tires of his manual chair. Maria had her hand resting casually over Julian’s on his armrest, her thumb gently tracing the familiar knuckles. It was a silent, continuous tactile tether that kept the anxiety of the impending international flight at bay.

"<lang="fr-CA" trans="Are you ready for the plane, Julep?">Tu es prêt pour l'avion, Julep?</lang>" Maria asked softly in flawless Québécois French, her voice barely rising above the hum of the van's heavy tires against the highway.

Julian didn't reach for his AAC device. He just looked at her, his eyes bright with a mix of intense, nervous energy and profound excitement, and offered a sharp, definitive nod. He reached up with his free hand and tapped the heavy, professional-grade isolation headphones resting around his neck. He had his armor. He was ready.

In the back row of the van, the nineteen-year-old twins were already operating in full tactical deployment mode. Gabriel had his broad shoulders slouched against the window, but his eyes were constantly scanning the highway traffic. Beside him, Gwendolyn had a thick, three-ring binder open on her lap. It was the master itinerary, heavily highlighted and tabbed, containing the exact layouts for Richmond International, the nightmare transfer at JFK, and the arrival terminals at Manchester.

"Flight 412 out of RIC is on time," Gwendolyn announced, her Welsh lilt crisp and commanding. "Richard, if we maintain this speed, we hit the Sandston exit in twelve minutes. Gabriel, you and I are on baggage duty the second the doors open. We leave the heavy lifting to the skycaps for the checked bags, but the wheelchair gate-check tag is our primary objective."

"I've got it covered, Gwen," Gabriel rumbled, his deep baritone projecting an unshakeable, frontline confidence. "Nobody touches his chair until we clear it."

Up front, the patriarch of the blended family gripped the steering wheel. Richard Harrison navigated the heavy vehicle with the quiet, effortless reliability that had made him the mortar of their family. He glanced at the rearview mirror, checking on the teenagers in the back, before looking over at his wife in the passenger seat.

Eira was staring out the passenger window, watching the dense, green Virginia pine trees blur past. She was dressed sharply for travel, her British passport and her U.S. Permanent Resident card securely zipped into her leather crossbody bag. But her fingers were tapping a restless, anxious rhythm against her knee.

It had been nine years since she had stepped foot on the Isle of Anglesey. She had left a crumbling marriage and a volatile life to try and force an American dream that had ultimately turned into a nightmare.

But she wasn't returning as a defeated exile. She was returning with Vanguard Horizon royalty money in the bank, a beautiful, fully accessible stone cottage waiting near the red cave, a fiercely devoted husband by her side, and all five of her children completely, legally safe.

"<lang="cy" trans="We are almost there, Eira,">Rydyn ni bron yno, Eira,</lang>" Richard murmured softly, switching seamlessly into her native tongue to soothe her racing mind.

Eira stopped tapping her knee. She reached across the center console, resting her hand warmly over Richard's on the steering wheel. She let out a long, shuddering exhale, a brilliant smile finally breaking through her travel anxiety.

"<lang="cy" trans="We are going home,">Rydyn ni'n mynd adref,</lang>" Eira whispered back.

Up ahead, the dense tree line finally broke. The towering, brutalist concrete structure of the Richmond International Airport air traffic control tower pierced the sweltering summer sky over Sandston.

Gabriel sat forward in the back row, his posture instantly straightening. Gwendolyn snapped her tactical binder shut with a definitive clack. In the center of the van, Julian pulled his heavy isolation headphones up over his ears, drowning out the world and plunging himself into absolute, predictable silence.

The perimeter was locked. The garrison was officially deploying.

***

#### 10:30 AM • Eira Rowlands — The Pre-Clearance

The automatic sliding doors of Richmond International Airport parted, and the Rowlands-Harrison garrison stepped out of the suffocating, 90-degree Virginia heat and into the echoing, air-conditioned expanse of the departure terminal.

For a typical family, the airport was just a stressful preamble to a vacation. But for Julian, the terminal was a sensory minefield. The high ceilings bounced the chaotic, overlapping sounds of thousands of people in a dozen different directions. The sharp clack-clack of hard plastic suitcase wheels rolling over the terrazzo floors, the blaring, distorted overhead announcements, and the chaotic hum of stressed travelers formed a suffocating wall of noise.

Julian reached up and pulled his heavy, Vanguard Horizon-funded isolation headphones firmly over his ears.

The chaotic roar of the terminal instantly vanished, reduced to a dull, manageable murmur. He let out a long, grounding exhale, his shoulders dropping an inch.

"I've got the cart," Richard announced, grabbing a heavy metal luggage trolley from the rack near the doors.

Gabriel effortlessly hoisted their three massive, scuffed canvas suitcases onto the trolley, stacking them with the precision of a frontline quartermaster.

"Formation," Gwendolyn ordered softly.

They didn't need to practice it. Clara stepped flush against Julian's left wheel, her hand resting over his. Maria took his right, her shoulder brushing against his arm. Gabriel pushed the luggage cart directly in front of them, acting as a physical snowplow to force oblivious travelers to part ways, while Eira and Gwendolyn took the lead, heading straight for the international ticketing desk of Meridian Airlines.

When they reached the front of the queue, the Meridian ticket agent offered a polite, professional smile. "Checking in for Flight 412 to New York, connecting to Manchester?"

Eira unzipped her leather crossbody bag. She reached inside and pulled out a thick, uniform stack of seven dark-blue United States passports, sliding them across the laminate counter.

Julian, watching closely from his wheelchair, frowned. A sudden, sharp spike of anxiety hit his chest. He pulled one side of his headphones back just enough to hear, his fingers quickly finding the keys of his AAC device.

<aac>WHY THE AMERICAN ONES?</aac> the robotic voice asked, the volume kept low but the urgency undeniable. <aac>WE ARE GOING HOME. WE ARE WELSH.</aac>

To Julian's incredibly literal, traumatized mind, using the American passports felt like a betrayal. It felt like holding onto the identity of Thomas Cross, instead of claiming the heritage that had literally saved his life.

Eira paused, turning away from the desk to look at her son. She understood the panic instantly. She didn't dismiss it.

"<lang="cy" trans="It's alright, Julep,">Mae'n iawn, Julep,</lang>" Eira whispered, stepping close to his chair and speaking in their native tongue so the ticket agent wouldn't understand. "<lang="cy" trans="This is only for the airline.">Dim ond i'r cwmni hedfan yw hyn.</lang>"

"She's right, Julian," Gwendolyn added, leaning in. "The airline agent just needs to match the names on the tickets to a valid photo ID to let us on the plane. But when we land in Manchester, we are walking right up to the UK Border desk, and we are handing them our burgundy British passports. We enter our country as citizens. I promise."

Julian stared at his sister, processing the tactical logic. The American passport wasn't an identity; it was just a boarding pass. He let out a quiet sigh, nodding once, and slipped the earcup of his headphones back into place.

Eira turned back to the agent, sliding a pristine, red-tabbed manila folder across the counter alongside the passports.

"Six checked bags," Eira stated briskly. "And the medical accommodation clearance for the manual wheelchair. I spoke with your accessibility director, Mr. Vance, on Tuesday. The profile is already noted in our booking."

The agent opened the folder, her eyes scanning the pre-approved airline forms and the letter from Julian's pediatrician. She typed rapidly into her terminal, pulling up the family's reservation.

"Ah, yes. I see it right here, Mrs. Rowlands," the agent confirmed, her tone shifting into one of profound professional respect. She didn't offer a generic airport transport chair; she simply reached under her desk and pulled out a bright, neon-orange gate-check tag attached to an elastic string.

She scribbled the flight number on it and handed it directly to Gabriel, along with the printed boarding passes.

"Gate-check is cleared. Just leave the chair at the end of the jet bridge, and the ramp crew at JFK has already been notified to bring it back up for the layover."

"Thank you," Gabriel said, offering a polite, appreciative nod.

He walked back to Julian and carefully looped the bright orange tag around the heavy metal crossbar under the seat of the manual wheelchair, pulling the elastic tight.

Julian looked down at the neon tag. To anyone else in the airport, it was just a piece of cheap cardboard. But to Julian, it was a profound symbol of his family's absolute, Frictionless competence. The world was chaotic, but his perimeter was bulletproof.

"Alright, Palace," Gabriel said, clapping his hands together once as Richard took control of the boarding passes. "The luggage is gone, and the chair is secured. Next stop: the metal detectors."

Julian squeezed Maria and Clara's hands. The perimeter moved forward.

***

#### 10:45 AM • Gabriel Rowlands — The Checkpoint

Airport security was a loud, clattering bottleneck of private security contractors, rattling plastic bins, and the monotonous, electronic beep of the walk-through magnetometers.

The chaotic chokepoint was a sensory minefield. Businessmen in oversized suits impatiently checked their pagers, and families wrestled with bulky carry-on bags. For Julian, even with his heavy isolation headphones firmly in place, the visual noise of the security lane was incredibly overwhelming.

The garrison moved to the front of the line, immediately executing their established protocol.

Gabriel and Richard stepped forward to handle the physical logistics. They grabbed a stack of gray plastic bins, rapidly emptying their pockets, removing their watches, and loading the family's carry-on duffels onto the rubber rollers of the X-ray belt.

Eira stepped up to the metal detector, presenting the boarding passes to the private security guard manning the archway.

"The boy in the wheelchair," the security guard said, gesturing lazily with a handheld metal-detecting wand. "He's going to set off the archway in that thing. Can he stand and walk through? If not, I need to wand him down and inspect the chair cushions."

"He cannot bear any weight on his legs, and he is severely autistic," Eira stated, her voice projecting a calm, absolute, and zero-tolerance authority. She didn't ask for permission; she dictated the terms of engagement. "He cannot be touched by a stranger, or he will go into severe muscular spasticity. My daughter will carry him through the archway, and my son will bring the empty chair around for your inspection."

The guard blinked, momentarily taken aback by the frictionless, military precision of the mother's instructions. He nodded, stepping aside to open the hinged bypass gate next to the metal detector. "Alright, ma'am. Bring the chair through here."

"<lang="fr-CA" trans="Come on, Julep. It's time,">Allez, Julep. C'est l'heure,</lang>" Gwendolyn murmured, stepping to the front of his wheelchair.

Julian hated leaving the safety of his customized seat in public, but he trusted his sister implicitly. He unclasped his seatbelt and leaned forward slightly.

Gwendolyn possessed a flawless, practiced mechanical leverage. She wrapped her arms securely around his torso and under his knees, lifting the fifteen-year-old boy out of the chair with a smooth, effortless exertion of core strength. She cradled him securely against her chest, her steady heartbeat providing an immediate, grounding rhythm against his ribs.

The moment Julian was airborne, his anchors moved in.

Maria stepped flush against his right side, and Clara against his left. They didn't just walk next to him; Maria reached up and wrapped her hand securely around his right hand, while Clara locked her fingers through his left.

"<lang="fr-CA" trans="We are here,">On est là,</lang>" Maria whispered, keeping her eyes locked on his.

Together, the three girls walked through the metal detector archway in perfect, synchronized tandem. Julian was entirely suspended, unable to touch the floor, but because he was physically tethered to Gwendolyn, Clara, and Maria, his unmedicated nervous system remained completely calm. He didn't flinch. The perimeter held him together.

Whoosh. They passed through the archway in absolute silence. No alarms. No red lights.

Immediately behind them, Gabriel pushed the empty manual wheelchair through the bypass gate. The security guard ran the handheld wand quickly over the seat and the backrest, glancing under the frame.

"Looks good. You're clear," the guard confirmed, stepping away.

Gabriel didn't wait a single second. He pushed the chair directly behind the metal detector, locking the brakes into place. Gwendolyn smoothly pivoted and lowered Julian back into his customized seat.

The moment Julian's weight settled back into the familiar, contoured cushions, he let out a long, heavy exhale. He reached down and clicked his seatbelt securely into place. Maria immediately adjusted the dark navy Aran wool sweater over his chest, while Clara ensured his heavy headphones were still perfectly positioned.

"Well done, everyone," Richard praised quietly, gathering their cleared duffel bags from the end of the X-ray belt and passing them out.

Gabriel grabbed the push-handles of Julian's chair. As they walked away from the clattering security checkpoint and merged into the wider, carpeted concourse of the domestic departure gates, the suffocating tension of the morning finally began to evaporate.

The Richmond departure lounge felt infinitely calmer. Sunlight poured through the massive, floor-to-ceiling windows, illuminating the tarmac where a sleek, silver-and-blue twin-engine jetliner belonging to the regional coastal carrier was parked at Gate B4.

Julian pulled his headphones down around his neck. The ambient noise here was a quiet, manageable hum. He looked out the window at the jet, a sudden, thrilling spark of adrenaline hitting his chest.

He wasn't trapped in the back of a freezing car. He wasn't hiding in his bedroom.

He pulled his AAC device onto his lap, his fingers flying across the keys with a bright, eager energy. He selected the Welsh synthesizer, the language of the home he hadn't seen in nine years.

<aac><lang="cy" trans="ARE WE FLYING NOW?">RYDYN NI'N HEDFAN NAWR?</lang></aac>

Gabriel chuckled, a massive, genuine smile breaking across his face as he parked the chair near the boarding door.

"<lang="cy" trans="Yes, brother,">Ydyn, brawd,</lang>" Gabriel confirmed, clapping a heavy hand affectionately onto Julian's shoulder. " Rydyn ni'n hedfan nawr. "

Eira stood beside Richard, looking out at the aircraft, her eyes shining with unshed tears of profound relief. The hardest logistical hurdles of the morning were behind them. They had successfully navigated the ticket counters, the luggage scales, and the security gauntlet without a single breach in their armor.

Next stop: New York. And then, the Menai Strait.

***

#### 11:30 AM • Julian Rowlands — The Ascent

The transition from the customized wheelchair into the narrow, fabric-upholstered airline seat was handled with the same frictionless, military precision the family applied to everything else.

Gwendolyn carried Julian down the jet bridge, transferring him seamlessly into the middle seat of Row 12. Gabriel immediately took the empty manual chair back up the ramp, ensuring the baggage handlers saw the neon-orange tag and loaded it securely into the forward cargo hold.

By the time the heavy cabin door sealed shut and the twin-engine jet pushed back from Gate B4, the perimeter was perfectly established.

Julian sat in the middle seat, his heavy isolation headphones resting around his neck. Maria sat in the window seat to his right, while Clara took the aisle on his left. Directly behind them, Gabriel and Gwendolyn formed the rear guard, and across the aisle, Richard and Eira kept a watchful eye on the formation.

As the jet taxied toward the runway, the low, mechanical whine of the engines began to vibrate through the floorboards. Julian reached out, firmly grasping Maria’s hand with his right hand, and Clara’s with his left.

"<lang="fr-CA" trans="Here we go,">Ici on y va,</lang>" Maria whispered in Québécois French, offering a bright, excited smile as the plane turned onto the active runway.

The jet engines roared, a massive, deafening surge of power that pushed Julian firmly back into his seat. As the aircraft accelerated down the tarmac and the nose lifted sharply into the sweltering Virginia sky, the intense G-force pressed down on his chest.

For Julian, the heavy gravitational pull didn't trigger his anxiety. It felt exactly like a massive, full-body deep-pressure cuddle. His unmedicated muscles relaxed entirely into the incline of the climb.

He looked out the small oval window past Maria, watching the sprawling, green tidewater of James City County rapidly shrink into a patchwork of tiny neighborhoods and winding rivers.

As the ground fell away, a sudden, vivid memory surfaced in his mind, piercing through the noise of the cabin.

He had not been on an airplane since he was six years old.

He remembered the flight out of the United Kingdom, nine years ago. He remembered sitting in a much larger airplane, his small legs dangling over the edge of the seat. But mostly, he remembered the man sitting next to him.

Tom Cross hadn't been a monster back then. He had been a smiling, sharply dressed father, holding his six-year-old son's hand as they flew across the Atlantic. Tom had leaned over, his voice devoid of the terrifying, paranoid rage that would eventually consume him, and promised Julian that America was going to be a grand, beautiful adventure.

It was a profound, deeply complicated memory. For years, Tom's escalating abuse had completely overwritten the man he used to be. The abduction had violently severed whatever remaining illusion of a father-son bond existed.

But sitting in Row 12, rocketing up to thirty thousand feet, Julian didn't feel paralyzed by the memory. He felt an overwhelming, undeniable sense of closure.

The American adventure his father had promised had shattered. The promises were empty, and the man who made them was dead.

But out of that wreckage, Julian hadn't just survived. He had gained Richard, who sat across the aisle reading a paperback novel, completely unfazed by the turbulence. He had gained Clara and Maria, who were currently anchoring his hands, perfectly in sync with his breathing. He had watched Gabriel and Gwendolyn forge themselves into unbreakable shields of steel.

Julian looked away from the window, turning his head to look at Clara on his left.

He didn't need to pull his AAC device onto his lap to communicate. He just squeezed her hand twice, offering a brilliant, radiant smile that completely reached his eyes.

"<lang="cy" trans="We are going home, Julep,">Rydyn ni'n mynd adref, Julep,</lang>" Clara whispered in flawless Welsh, instantly reading the overwhelming joy on his face.

Julian let out a happy, rhythmic vocalization that was completely swallowed by the ambient roar of the jet engines. He pulled his heavy headphones up over his ears, leaning his head against Maria’s shoulder, and closed his eyes.

He wasn't flying into the terrifying unknown with a man who refused to learn his mother's language. He was flying back to the very roots of his sanctuary, completely surrounded by a garrison that spoke his language perfectly.

The flight to New York’s JFK airport was a quick, ninety-minute hop up the eastern seaboard. It was just enough time for Julian's nervous system to completely reset before they had to face the ultimate, chaotic gauntlet of the international transfer terminal.

### Part 2: The Hub & The Red-Eye

**Date:** 1998-08-02 at 13:00 America/New_York

#### 1:00 PM • Gwendolyn Rowlands — The Hub

The wheels of the twin-engine jet hit the tarmac at John F. Kennedy International Airport with a heavy, shuddering thud, the thrust reversers roaring as the aircraft rapidly decelerated.

Julian kept his eyes closed, his hands firmly anchored in Maria and Clara’s grips. The sudden deceleration pushed him forward against his seatbelt, but the familiar, symmetrical pressure on his hands kept his unmedicated nervous system grounded.

When the seatbelt sign finally chimed off, the cabin erupted into the usual frantic, claustrophobic scramble of passengers reaching for the overhead bins.

The garrison didn't move an inch.

Gabriel stood up into the aisle, his broad shoulders effectively blocking anyone from crowding Row 12. "Hold the line," he rumbled quietly. "We wait for the chair."

Ten minutes later, the cabin had emptied. Gwendolyn effortlessly lifted Julian, carrying him down the jet bridge where Gabriel was already waiting with the customized manual wheelchair. The bright neon-orange gate-check tag was still securely tied to the crossbar.

Gwendolyn lowered him into the seat, and Julian immediately felt the tension bleed out of his spine. The airplane seat had been tolerable, but his chair was an extension of his own body. It was his mobile fortress.

"Alright, Palace," Richard said, checking his watch as he took the lead. "We are in Terminal 2. Our flight to Manchester leaves out of the International Arrivals Building, which handles the foreign carrier departures. We have to take the curbside shuttle."

Eira nodded, her expression hardening into absolute, tactical focus. "Headphones on, Julep. It's going to be loud."

Julian pulled the heavy isolation cups securely over his ears.

They marched up the jet bridge and into the terminal. JFK was not a quiet, regional airport. It was a massive, echoing cavern of stressed, shouting travelers, blaring announcements, and rushing electric carts. But the real assault began when they bypassed the baggage claims—since their luggage was checked through to Manchester—and pushed through the automatic doors onto the curbside pickup lane.

The August heat in New York was punishing, radiating off the concrete in shimmering, exhaust-choked waves. The smell of burnt jet fuel, hot asphalt, and diesel smoke was instantly suffocating.

A heavy, articulated shuttle bus hissed to a stop at the curb, its doors folding open with a loud, mechanical clatter.

"Ramp!" Gabriel shouted over the din of the traffic, catching the attention of the bus driver.

The driver stepped out of his seat, deploying a heavy metal plate onto the curb. Gabriel didn't wait for assistance. He expertly navigated Julian’s chair up the steep incline and into the designated wheelchair bay on the bus. He engaged the chair's brakes and immediately locked the heavy, yellow floor tie-downs onto the frame.

Gwendolyn, Richard, and Eira filed in, while Clara and Maria immediately took the flip-down seats directly beside Julian's wheels.

The doors hissed shut, and the bus lurched forward.

For Julian, the ten-minute ride around the perimeter of JFK was a sensory nightmare, even with the headphones blocking the noise. The bus rattled violently over the uneven tarmac, the suspension groaning at every turn. The heavy smell of diesel fumes seeped through the rubber seals of the doors, making him nauseous.

He squeezed his eyes shut, his hands balling into fists on his armrests. The phantom sway of a moving vehicle was clawing at the edges of his mind, threatening to drag him back to the dark, freezing roads of Ohio.

A hand gently covered his left fist. He opened his eyes.

Clara was leaning forward, her face mere inches from his. She didn't speak—she knew he couldn't hear her through the thick foam of the headphones—but she offered a slow, deliberate, and incredibly exaggerated smile.

On his right, Maria tapped his knee twice, holding up her hand and counting down on her fingers. Three. Two. One. The bus lurched to a halt, the air brakes hissing loudly.

"We're here," Gabriel announced, moving swiftly to unclip the floor restraints. "Let's move."

They rolled down the ramp and pushed through a set of towering glass doors into the International Departures Hall.

If the curbside was a sensory assault, the international terminal was a sprawling, echoing metropolis. It was a kaleidoscope of humanity. Tens of thousands of people swarmed the check-in desks, speaking dozens of different languages. The vaulted ceilings trapped the noise, creating a continuous, low-frequency roar.

But Eira Rowlands did not falter. She was entirely in her element. She moved with a crisp, undeniable authority, her eyes locked on the massive, mechanical flip-board high above the concourse that clattered rapidly as it updated gate numbers.

"Gate 4," Eira directed, pointing down a long, crowded corridor. "Richard, Gabriel, clear the path."

The two men moved to the front of the formation. They didn't push or shove, but their combined size and determined stride caused the crowds of international travelers to naturally part like the Red Sea.

Gwendolyn took the push-handles of Julian's chair, maintaining a brisk, smooth pace, while the twins kept their shoulders flush against the wheels, physically blocking any stray luggage carts from getting too close.

They bypassed the duty-free shops and the crowded terminal bars, marching relentlessly until the dense crowds finally began to thin out near the end of the concourse.

Gate 4 was a spacious, carpeted waiting area with floor-to-ceiling windows looking out over the massive wingspan of a wide-body, transatlantic jetliner.

Gwendolyn wheeled Julian into a quiet corner against the glass, pivoting the chair so his back was to the wall. He had a clear, unobstructed view of the entire gate area, and no one could approach him from behind.

"Perimeter secured," Gwendolyn said, letting out a sharp exhale as she dropped her heavy carry-on bag onto the carpet.

Julian pulled his headphones down to rest around his neck. The ambient noise here was manageable. He looked up at his family. They were all sweating, their clothes rumpled from the oppressive heat of the shuttle bus, but their eyes were bright with a fierce, collective triumph.

They had survived the hub.

Julian pulled his AAC device onto his lap. His hands were shaking slightly from the adrenaline of the transit, but he navigated effortlessly to the Welsh synthesizer.

<aac><lang="cy" trans="JFK IS TOO LOUD.">MAE JFK YN DWEUD Y RHYW BETH.</lang></aac>

Gabriel let out a booming, exhausted laugh, collapsing into one of the vinyl airport chairs. "<lang="cy" trans="You are exactly right, brother.">Rydych chi'n llygad eich lle, brawd,</lang>" "It's a madhouse."

Eira stepped up to the window, placing her hand gently on Julian's shoulder. She looked out at the massive international jet sitting on the tarmac, the ground crews actively loading the cargo holds for the long flight over the Atlantic.

"It's loud, Julep," Eira agreed softly, her British accent returning to its natural, unhurried cadence now that the frantic rush was over. "But that plane right there? That is the very last step. When we get off that plane, we will be standing in Manchester."

Julian looked out the window at the jet. He reached out, his fingers finding Maria's hand.

He didn't need to type anything else. He was ready to go home.

***

#### 6:15 PM • Eira Rowlands — The Boarding

The static crackle of the public address system echoed through the International Departures Hall, followed by the gate agent's voice announcing the pre-boarding process for passengers requiring extra time or special assistance.

Gabriel immediately hoisted his heavy canvas duffel onto his shoulder, offering Richard a sharp, ready nod. The garrison was moving out.

But in the center of their formation, Julian’s manual wheelchair jerked to a sudden, rigid halt.

Julian’s hands began patting his chest, then his lap, his eyes going wide with a sudden, frantic spike of panic. He looked toward Eira’s leather crossbody bag, his breathing turning shallow and rapid. He pulled his AAC device squarely onto his lap, his fingers hitting the keys with urgent, heavy strikes.

<aac>PASSPORT.</aac> the robotic voice barked, the volume cutting through the ambient hum of the terminal. <aac>I DO NOT HAVE MY PASSPORT.</aac>

He remembered the ticketing desk in Richmond. He remembered his mother sliding the stack of dark-blue booklets across the counter to the agent. To his literal, anxious mind, the gate door was another insurmountable border. If he didn't have his passport in his hand right now, they were going to leave him behind.

Before the panic could fully trigger a muscular spasm, Gwendolyn was there.

She dropped her carry-on bag onto the carpet and immediately knelt directly in front of his wheelchair, bringing herself perfectly into his line of sight. She didn't try to reason with him from a distance or tell him to calm down. She stepped directly into his space, wrapping both of her arms securely around his chest and pulling him into a firm, unyielding deep-pressure hug.

"<lang="fr-CA" trans="Quiet, Julep,">Tranquille, Julep,</lang>" Gwendolyn murmured, her voice an incredibly soft, grounding anchor amidst the chaos of the airport. She held him tightly, her steady heartbeat pressing against his ribs.

Julian gasped, his rigid shoulders fighting the hold for only a fraction of a second before completely melting into his older sister's embrace. He buried his face against her shoulder, his trembling hands gripping the fabric of her sweater.

"<lang="cy" trans="You do not need the passport for the plane, love,">Nid oes angen y pasbort arnoch chi ar gyfer yr awyren, cariad,</lang>" Gwendolyn explained in a low, soothing Welsh lilt, rocking him gently. "We just need the paper boarding pass to get on the aircraft. That's it. Mom has your British passport zipped away, completely safe. You will hold it tomorrow morning when we land, and you will hand it to the guard to go home. I promise you are not being left behind."

Julian let out a long, shuddering exhale against her shoulder. The terrifying logic puzzle in his mind had been solved. He squeezed her tightly one last time before leaning back, offering a small, exhausted nod.

Gwendolyn smiled, smoothing a stray piece of hair from his forehead. " Allez. Let's get on this plane."

The transition down the jet bridge was executed flawlessly. Gwendolyn seamlessly transferred Julian from his customized chair into the middle seat of Row 12, while Gabriel parked the manual wheelchair at the threshold of the aircraft door, making absolutely certain the baggage handlers saw the neon-orange gate-check tag before he stepped inside.

Within minutes, the perimeter was locked. Julian sat securely in his seat, his heavy isolation headphones resting comfortably around his neck, perfectly flanked by Maria on his right and Clara on his left. Across the aisle, Eira settled into her window seat, leaning her head against Richard’s shoulder with a profound, exhausted sigh of relief.

Directly behind Julian, Gabriel and Gwendolyn slid into Row 13.

The heavy, reinforced cabin door was pulled shut with a definitive, mechanical thud. The ambient noise of the airport vanished, replaced by the deep, resonant hum of the wide-body jet's auxiliary power unit and the rush of the overhead air conditioning.

Gabriel stowed his duffel under the seat in front of him and sat back. He let out a massive, heavy breath, the intense, volatile frontline energy that had kept his muscles coiled like springs all day finally, completely bleeding out of his jaw and shoulders.

Beside him, Gwendolyn closed her tactical binder, slipping it into the seatback pocket.

The nineteen-year-old twins looked at each other. They didn't need to speak. They had spent their entire childhoods as hyper-vigilant human shields, constantly scanning their environment for the heavy footsteps of their father, and later, standing as the impenetrable wall that kept the world from overwhelming their little brother.

But right now, the doors were sealed. The threat was entirely gone.

Gabriel shifted the armrest between them up and out of the way. He slid closer to the middle seat, wrapping his heavy, broad arm securely around his twin sister's shoulders.

Gwendolyn didn't hesitate. The brilliant, calculating tactician immediately let her guard down, leaning her entire body weight against Gabriel's side. She rested her head comfortably against his chest, tucking her feet up onto the edge of her seat, and closed her eyes.

It was a quiet, domestic cuddle—the exact same way they slept every night in their shared bedroom in Virginia to keep the ghosts at bay. But here, sitting on a transatlantic jetliner taxiing toward the runway, the embrace felt infinitely lighter.

Gabriel rested his chin against the top of her head, his eyes tracking the tarmac out the window. They were flying over the ocean, leaving the remnants of Thomas Cross's America entirely behind them. They were going back to the cobblestones, the cold salt air, and the language of their roots.

" Rydyn ni'n mynd adref, " Gabriel whispered into his sister's hair.

Gwendolyn smiled against his shirt, her breathing slowing to match his steady, rhythmic pulse. They were finally going home.

***

#### 9:30 PM • Julian Rowlands — The Red-Eye

The interior of the wide-body jet underwent a slow, deliberate transformation as it reached cruising altitude high over the dark expanse of the Atlantic Ocean.

The flight attendants moved quietly down the aisles, collecting the remnants of the dinner service, before the harsh overhead cabin lights finally clicked off. They were replaced by the soft, dim glow of the floor runners and the scattered, pale beams of individual reading lights.

The chaotic, frantic energy of the departure had completely dissipated. The only sound left was the continuous, heavy, white-noise roar of the massive jet engines outside the fuselage.

Across the aisle from the teenagers, Richard Harrison rested his head against the high back of his seat. He had an open paperback novel resting on his lap, but he hadn't turned a page in over twenty minutes. He was staring out the small oval window into the pitch-black void of the night sky.

At forty-two years old, Richard had never left the United States. His entire life had been defined by the borders of Virginia—teaching music therapy in the local public schools, raising his twin daughters after his first wife passed, and eventually navigating the corporate broadcasting ladder.

But as he looked at the dark glass, the sheer magnitude of what they were doing settled heavily into his chest. He wasn't just taking a vacation. He was crossing an ocean to help his wife reclaim a homeland that had been stolen from her, and to bring a traumatized fifteen-year-old boy back to the only place he had ever truly felt safe.

Eira shifted in the window seat beside him, letting out a soft, exhausted sigh as she rested her head against his shoulder.

"You're thinking incredibly loudly, Richard," Eira murmured, her voice thick with the creeping pull of sleep.

Richard smiled, wrapping his arm securely around her. "Just looking at the dark. I've never been over the Atlantic before."

"It looks exactly the same as the dark over Virginia," Eira whispered, her eyes already closed. "But tomorrow morning, the sun is going to come up over the Irish Sea. And I'm going to show you the mountains."

"I can't wait," Richard said quietly, kissing the top of her head. He looked over his shoulder. In Row 13, the formidable frontline shields had finally powered down. Gabriel was fast asleep, his head tilted back against the headrest, while Gwendolyn was curled up against his side, breathing in a slow, steady rhythm.

But in Row 12, the battle for rest was still being fought.

Julian was exhausted. The sheer sensory toll of the sweltering, chaotic transfer at the New York terminal was still echoing in his mind. Physically, he was safe; the preventative dose of muscle relaxants his pediatrician had prescribed for the flight was doing its job perfectly, keeping his legs from locking into the agonizing, rigid spasticity that usually accompanied cramped spaces.

But chemical relaxation doesn't instantly erase psychological adrenaline. His unmedicated mind was still racing, the phantom smells of diesel and the echoing shouts of the terminal playing on a loop beneath his heavy isolation headphones. His hands were clenched into tight fists on his lap, his breathing shallow and rapid.

He needed his anchors.

Maria and Clara didn't need to ask what was wrong. They could feel the vibrating tension radiating from his shoulders. The cramped economy seats made a proper, full-body hold difficult, but the identical twins had spent years adapting to Julian's exact needs.

Clara unbuckled her seatbelt just enough to shift her weight. She leaned over the fixed armrest, pressing her side entirely flush against Julian's left arm. She reached across his lap, taking his tightly clenched left fist and sandwiching it firmly between both of her hands, applying a steady, crushing pressure.

On his right, Maria mirrored the movement perfectly. She leaned her head heavily onto Julian's shoulder, wrapping her arm securely around his back to press against his spine. She took his right fist, interlocking her fingers with his and squeezing with a fierce, unyielding gravity.

"<lang="fr-CA" trans="Let yourself go, Julep,">Laisse-toi aller, Julep,</lang>" Maria whispered in Québécois French, her lips right near the edge of his foam earcup so he could just barely hear the soothing, rhythmic cadence of her voice. "<lang="fr-CA" trans="We are here. No one can reach you.">On est là. Personne ne peut t'atteindre.</lang>"

"<lang="cy" trans="Sleep peacefully,">Cysga yn dawel,</lang>" Clara added from his left, the ancient, flowing Welsh syllables wrapping around him like a second blanket.

Julian gasped, his chest heaving against the seatbelt as the symmetrical, deep-pressure cuddle finally short-circuited the residual panic loop. The physical sensation of his two best friends pressing against him from both sides replaced the chaotic memories of the airport.

He focused entirely on the heavy, synchronized pressure on his hands. Slowly, the remaining tension in his shoulders melted away. His fists unclenched entirely, his fingers relaxing into the twins' grips.

He let his head loll to the right, resting it heavily against Maria's hair.

The perimeter was impenetrable. The airplane was hurtling through the freezing upper atmosphere at six hundred miles an hour, but in Row 12, suspended somewhere between the trauma of the United States and the promise of the Menai Strait, Julian Rowlands finally, completely let go.

His breathing slowed, matching the deep, ambient rumble of the jet engines, and he fell asleep.

## The Red Cave

### Part 1: The Terminator Line & The Commuter Hop

**Date:** 1998-08-03 at 07:00 Europe/London

#### 7:00 AM • Julian Rowlands — The Terminator Line

Richard Harrison had not slept a single minute.

He sat perfectly still in the aisle seat of Row 12, his open paperback novel entirely forgotten on his lap. For the past four hours, he had kept his eyes locked on the small, oval window across the aisle.

When they had departed New York, the sky outside was a suffocating, pitch-black void. But because the heavy, wide-body jet was hurtling eastward across the Atlantic at six hundred miles per hour, they were physically chasing the morning.

The transition, when it finally began, did not happen slowly.

First, a razor-thin, vibrating line of bruised purple appeared on the edge of the curvature of the Earth. Within minutes, the purple ignited into a fiercely glowing, radioactive streak of atomic orange. The colors didn't lazily stretch across the horizon like they did over the flatlands of Virginia; they boiled and expanded with unnatural, accelerated speed, completely flooding the cabin with a sharp, unfiltered, brilliant light.

Eira stirred in the window seat beside him. She let out a soft groan, shielding her eyes with the back of her hand against the sudden, piercing glare of the high-altitude dawn.

"Good morning," Richard whispered gently, reaching over to lower the plastic window shade halfway to cut the glare.

Eira blinked, sitting up and looking out at the sprawling, endless expanse of white, cotton-like clouds stretching out below them, painted entirely in gold and amber.

"<lang="cy" trans="What is the time?">Sut mae'r amser?</lang>" she murmured, her voice thick with sleep as she rubbed her eyes.

"Just past seven in the morning, local time," Richard replied, a quiet sense of awe bleeding into his voice. "We're over the Irish Sea. The captain just announced the initial descent into Manchester."

Eira's posture instantly straightened. The exhaustion of the transatlantic crossing vanished from her face, replaced by a sharp, electric surge of adrenaline. She leaned all the way forward against her seatbelt, pressing her forehead almost flush against the thick acrylic glass of the window, peering down through the breaks in the cloud cover.

Across the aisle, the sudden influx of sunlight broke the perimeter's deep slumber.

Julian’s eyes fluttered open behind the thick rims of his glasses. For a terrifying, disorienting fraction of a second, the phantom memory of Ohio flickered in his mind. He remembered waking up in the freezing, rusted backseat of his father’s sedan, the pale, gray dawn illuminating the terrifying reality that he had been stolen.

But the panic couldn't take physical hold. The preventative muscle relaxants his pediatrician had prescribed for the flight were doing exactly what they were supposed to do, keeping his legs comfortably pliant and completely preventing the agonizing, rigid spasticity of the past.

And more importantly, his tactile anchors were right there to ground his racing mind.

His right hand was securely interwoven with Maria's. She was still fast asleep, her head resting heavily against his shoulder, her breathing slow and rhythmic. His left hand was entirely enveloped by Clara's warm, firm grip. She was already awake, her blue eyes watching him closely in the golden light of the cabin.

Clara smiled, giving his left hand a firm, deliberate squeeze. "<lang="cy" trans="Good morning, Julep,">Bore da, Julep,</lang>" she whispered in Welsh.

Julian let out a long, heavy exhale, sinking perfectly into the fabric of the airline seat. He wasn't in the car. He wasn't trapped. He squeezed her hand back, offering a soft, sleep-drunk smile.

He reached up with his free fingers and carefully pulled his heavy isolation headphones down around his neck. The ambient hum of the cabin was manageable, replaced by the quiet rustle of passengers beginning to wake up and stow their blankets.

"Julian," Eira called softly across the aisle.

Julian turned his head. His mother wasn't looking at him; her eyes were still fiercely locked onto the window. Her hands were trembling slightly where they rested against the plastic trim.

"Look," Eira whispered, her voice cracking with a profound, overwhelming emotion.

Julian leaned forward against his seatbelt, peering past his mother's shoulder and out the window.

The heavy, golden cloud cover was finally beginning to break apart as the jet descended toward the coastline. Below them, cutting through the swirling morning mist, the dark, jagged, ancient peaks of the Snowdonia mountain range pierced the sky. To the north, the deep, rushing, tidal waters of the Menai Strait glittered like shattered glass in the brilliant morning sun, separating the rugged Welsh mainland from the sprawling, green sanctuary of the Isle of Anglesey.

Julian stared at the coastline. He had not seen it since he was six years old.

He didn't reach for his AAC device. He didn't need to type the words. The sheer, overwhelming magnitude of the view hit him like a physical weight in his chest. A sudden, bright tear slipped out from behind his glasses, rolling silently down his cheek.

He was home.

In Row 13, Gabriel leaned forward, resting his heavy chin on the back of Julian's seat. He looked out the window at the rugged mountains, a massive, victorious grin breaking across his face.

"Alright, garrison," Gabriel rumbled, his deep baritone carrying the unyielding confidence of a general who had successfully delivered his troops across the ocean. "Time to pack up the perimeter. Next stop: the ground."

***

#### 7:45 AM • Eira Rowlands — The Border

The transition off the aircraft at Manchester Airport was the smoothest maneuver of the entire journey.

Because they were seated near the front of the economy cabin in Row 12, the wait was minimal. Gwendolyn lifted Julian from his seat with practiced ease, carrying him out the heavy cabin door and onto the cool, carpeted incline of the jet bridge. Gabriel was right behind her, retrieving the customized manual wheelchair from the baggage handlers who had brought it up from the cargo hold exactly as promised.

The moment Julian was lowered back into his own chair, he felt a profound, atmospheric shift.

The air bleeding into the jet bridge from the terminal didn't smell like the suffocating, exhaust-choked humidity of New York or the heavy pine of Virginia. It was cooler, damper, and carried the distinct, metallic tang of the British morning.

"Perimeter," Gabriel ordered softly, taking the push-handles of the chair.

Maria and Clara instantly took their positions on the left and right wheels. Richard led the way, holding three small, rectangular white paper Landing Cards he had filled out on the plane, while Eira walked directly beside Julian, her leather crossbody bag unzipped and ready.

The walk to the UK Immigration Hall was a long, echoing maze of glass corridors. Julian kept his headphones around his neck. The ambient noise of the Manchester terminal was different than America—the overlapping voices were quieter, the accents rolling in familiar, comforting cadences that he had only heard inside the walls of the Ewell house.

When they reached the massive Immigration Hall, they were met with two distinct queues: one marked UK/EU Passports, and a significantly longer, slower serpentine line marked All Other Passports.

Typically, mixed-nationality families were forced to split up or endure the agonizing wait of the foreign queue. But Eira Rowlands did not surrender her perimeter.

She marched the entire garrison straight up to the front of the empty UK/EU queue, stepping up to the glass partition of the Border Control desk.

The UK Border Force officer, a stern-looking man in a crisp white shirt and dark epaulets, looked up from his monitor. He saw a family of seven, a heavy-duty wheelchair, and a mix of complex travel documents.

"Morning, Officer," Eira said, her British accent instantly cutting through any bureaucratic hesitation. She didn't hand over a massive, confusing stack of documents. She controlled the narrative perfectly.

She slid four pristine, burgundy European Union/United Kingdom passports under the glass partition.

"Four British nationals returning home," Eira stated, pointing to herself, Gabriel, Gwendolyn, and then resting her hand gently on Julian's shoulder.

The officer took the four burgundy books. He opened Eira's first, checking her photo against her face. He scanned it, stamped it, and set it aside. He did the same for Gwendolyn and Gabriel.

Then, he opened the fourth passport. He looked down at the photo of the fifteen-year-old boy, and then looked over the desk at Julian.

Julian sat perfectly still in his chair. His mind was crystal clear, the preventative medication keeping him physically relaxed while the twins' hands resting on his wheels kept him psychologically grounded. He met the officer's eyes through the thick rims of his glasses. He wasn't hiding. He wasn't Thomas Cross's stolen American son.

"Welcome back, Mr. Rowlands," the officer said with a polite nod, sliding the burgundy passport back under the glass.

Julian’s breath hitched in his chest. It was the first time in nine years a government official had addressed him by his true name, in his own country. He reached down to his AAC device, his fingers trembling just slightly, and typed a single word using the English synthesizer.

<aac>THANKS.</aac>

The officer offered a small smile, then looked back at Eira. "And the rest of your party, ma'am?"

Eira slid the three dark-blue United States passports under the glass, along with the three completed paper Landing Cards.

"My husband, Richard, and my two stepdaughters, Maria and Clara," Eira explained smoothly. "They are American citizens traveling with us on holiday."

The officer opened Richard's passport. "Purpose and duration of your stay, sir?"

"Family holiday," Richard answered, his calm, steady voice projecting absolute reliability. "My wife just purchased a cottage in Llanfairpwll on Anglesey. We are staying for the month to get the house settled, and we fly back to New York on the second of September, right before the girls start their school year."

The officer reviewed the Landing Cards, ensuring the return date matched the story. A British citizen bringing her American husband and dependents home for a summer holiday at a verifiable address was the easiest clearance of the day.

Thump. Thump. Thump.

The officer brought a heavy, black ink stamp down on a blank page in each of the three American passports, granting them standard six-month visitor entry. He slid the blue books back under the glass.

"Right then," the officer said, gathering the landing cards. "Enjoy the new cottage in Wales, folks. You're all cleared through."

"Thank you," Eira breathed, a massive, profound wave of relief finally washing over her face. She gathered the passports, carefully zipping them back into her leather bag.

Gabriel pushed Julian’s chair past the glass booth and out through the final set of automatic doors into the chaotic, echoing expanse of the international baggage claim.

They had crossed the final legal threshold. There were no more checkpoints, no more ticket agents, and no more metal detectors.

Eira turned around to face her family. She looked at Richard, then at the twins, then at her three Welsh children.

"<lang="cy" trans="Welcome home,">Croeso adref,</lang>" Eira whispered, her eyes shining with unshed tears.

Gabriel let out a massive, booming whoop that echoed over the baggage carousels, pulling Gwendolyn into a sudden, bone-crushing hug. Richard laughed, wrapping his arms around his wife.

In the center of the celebration, Julian looked down at his lap. He pulled his AAC device close, deleting the English text and switching the synthesizer back to its permanent, rightful setting.

<aac><lang="cy" trans="IT IS TIME FOR THE TRAIN.">MAE'N AMSER YR CAR TREN.</lang></aac>

***

#### 8:15 AM • Julian Rowlands — The Commuter Hop

The international baggage claim at Manchester Airport was a cavernous, echoing hall of grinding conveyor belts and exhausted travelers, but the Rowlands-Harrison garrison moved through it with frictionless, military precision.

Gabriel and Richard stepped up to the edge of the metal carousel, claiming two heavy luggage trolleys. As the chute spat out their six massive, scuffed canvas suitcases, the two men hoisted them onto the trolleys with the effortless synchronization of a frontline quartermaster and a steadfast anchor.

A few feet away, safely out of the chaotic crush of the crowd, Julian sat in his customized manual wheelchair, perfectly flanked by Maria on his right and Clara on his left. The heavy isolation headphones still rested comfortably around his neck. The ambient noise of the British terminal was different than the suffocating roar of New York. The overlapping voices here carried the rolling, familiar cadences of his mother's side of the Atlantic, instantly lowering the defensive spikes in his unmedicated nervous system.

"Right then, garrison," Gabriel rumbled, his deep baritone carrying the crisp, rhythmic Welsh lilt of his heritage. He gripped the handle of the lead luggage trolley. "Bags are secured. Let's get to the platforms."

"The railway station is connected directly to the terminal via the Skylink walkway," Gwendolyn directed, her own British English clipping with tactical authority as she referenced the heavily tabbed itinerary in her binder. "We take the local commuter line straight into Manchester Piccadilly. We have a forty-five-minute layover there before we board the coastal line to Wales."

Eira nodded, her posture radiating a fierce, triumphant energy. "Lead the way, Gwen."

The transition from the international baggage claim to the railway station was the first time the garrison didn’t have to actively fight the environment for space.

Gabriel and Richard pushed the two towering luggage trolleys through the elevated, glass-walled Skylink walkway. As they left the heavily air-conditioned terminal behind, the climate shifted. The air filtering in from the sliding doors didn't carry the suffocating, exhaust-choked 90-degree heat of August in Virginia. It was crisp, damp, and carried the distinct, metallic chill of a northern English morning.

"Right, grab your coats if you need them," Gabriel directed, his deep baritone carrying the crisp, rhythmic Welsh lilt of his heritage. "It's a bit brisk on the platforms."

"Oh, I'll take this over the Richmond humidity any day of the week," Richard chuckled. His warm, Hampton Roads drawl provided a grounding, familiar contrast to the echoing British announcements overhead.

Gwendolyn walked point, her tactical binder tucked under her arm. "We take the local commuter line straight into Crewe. It's only a thirty-minute hop, but it gets us out of the airport hub. We have a forty-minute layover at Crewe to switch to the North Western Trains coastal service."

Julian sat securely in his customized manual wheelchair in the center of the formation, flanked flush by Maria and Clara. His heavy isolation headphones rested comfortably around his neck. The ambient noise here wasn't a threat; the overlapping voices echoing down the walkway carried the rolling, familiar cadences of his mother's side of the Atlantic, instantly lowering the defensive spikes in his unmedicated nervous system.

When they descended the lifts to the station platform, the logistics Eira had meticulously arranged weeks in advance instantly materialized. Because Julian's manual wheelchair required specialized boarding assistance, the Passenger Assist protocol was already active.

A uniformed conductor stood waiting by the open doors of the heavy, electric commuter train, a bright yellow folding ramp resting against the carriage threshold.

"Rowlands party?" the conductor asked, checking his clipboard.

"That's us. Spot on, thank you," Eira replied, her British accent instantly establishing a natural, frictionless rapport that had been entirely absent in the United States.

Gabriel pushed Julian’s chair smoothly up the incline and into the dedicated accessible bay. The environment inside the carriage was a profound, immediate upgrade from the cramped fuselage of the jetliner. There was breathing room.

Gabriel engaged the brakes on the wheelchair, ensuring it was securely locked into place against the bulkhead. Instead of being crammed side-by-side in narrow airline seats, Maria and Clara slid into the folding seats directly across from Julian. They didn't need to contort their shoulders to reach him; they simply reached across the small gap. Maria enveloped his right hand, and Clara anchored his left.

With a sharp blast of a whistle, the carriage doors hissed shut.

The electric train pulled away from the Manchester Airport station, accelerating smoothly out into the gray sprawl of the suburbs.

Julian let his head fall back against his headrest, a profound wave of relief washing over his face. The train did not swerve unpredictably like a stolen car on a terrifying Appalachian highway, and it didn't rattle violently like the airport shuttle bus at JFK. It was locked onto a fixed, undeniable path. The heavy, rhythmic clack-clack of the steel wheels vibrating through the floorboards acted as a massive, mechanical metronome, sending a deep-pressure vibration straight up through the frame of his chair and into his bones.

He hadn't been on a train since he was six years old, back before Tom Cross had uprooted them across the ocean. But as the carriage swayed gently with the curve of the tracks, a deep, buried somatic memory woke up inside him. The rails felt like childhood. They felt like the days before the screaming started.

"<lang="fr-CA" trans="It's perfect, right?">C'est parfait, non?</lang>" Maria smiled in Québécois French, offering a bright, exhausted smile as she felt the tension completely bleed out of his fingers. "<lang="fr-CA" trans="Much better than the plane.">Beaucoup mieux que l'avion.</lang>"

Julian pulled his AAC device squarely onto his lap. His hands were completely steady. He navigated past the Welsh and French dictionaries, deliberately selecting the British English synthesizer. He wanted to engage with the world outside the Ewell house on his own terms, using the vocabulary of his homeland.

<aac>BRILLIANT RIDE.</aac> The digitized voice spoke, the crisp, synthesized British accent perfectly matching the environment outside the window. <aac>JUST LIKE I REMEMBER. MUCH BETTER THAN THE LORRY AT THE AIRPORT.</aac>

Gabriel let out a booming laugh from the adjacent seat, recognizing Julian's playful jab at the rattling shuttle bus they had endured in New York.

"Just a quick warm-up ride to get your sea legs back, Julep," Gwendolyn said, offering a warm, relaxed smile.

For the Americans in the group, the linguistic dynamic had entirely flipped. Richard, Maria, and Clara's Hampton Roads accents suddenly marked them as the foreigners, while Julian, his mother, and his older siblings were flawlessly integrated into the background noise of the carriage. Julian let out a quiet, happy hum, his fingers tapping rhythmically against his armrest. His voice, his family, and his environment were finally completely synchronized.

"Crewe is a massive Victorian junction," Eira warned gently, leaning across the aisle. "It will be busy, Julian. But we're just moving from one platform to another to catch the coastal train."

Julian nodded. He didn't reach for his headphones. He just squeezed Maria and Clara's hands, watching the English countryside blur past the thick glass of the window.
